A now-former officer with the Central Intelligence Agency has pleaded guilty to stealing almost $200 million, including hundreds of gold bars, as part of a fraudulent scheme masquerading as a top secret government program. The U.S. Department of Justice on Tuesday confirmed that David J. Rush, a Virginia resident, admitted to one count of wire fraud linked to running the fabricated operation and defrauding the government while he was a CIA officer. In a statement, prosecutors said that Rush lied about his education and military experience to gain a position at the CIA, and used his access as an employee to set up the top secret and highly compartmentalized program with the intention of using it to funnel U.S. government funds into his possession. During his arrest, law enforcement found more than $46 million worth of gold bars in Rush’s house linked to the long-running fraud scheme, along with cars, watches, and over $2 million in cash. The plea deal means that Rush escapes trial, as much as the U.S. government likely avoids having to explain in open court how Rush evaded vetting scrutiny to join the U.S. intelligence agency, and how actions went undetected for more than a year. Rush was until recently a senior CIA officer, who according to earlier reports worked for a division of the CIA since 2010 that develops hacking tools and techniques for carrying out espionage. Per court filings, Rush had significant authority over government intelligence programs and spending. Over the course of 2025, Rush used his position to fabricate a so-called special access program, which are surveillance or intelligence-gathering operations so sensitive that only a few people are read into them. Special access programs, or SAPs, require special clearance that not even the most cleared individuals in government are granted access to. According to The Washington Post, Rush constructed the compartmentalized program under the guise of a fake “continuity of government” plan, aimed at keeping the government functioning in the event of a war, natural disaster, or some other destructive catastrophe. As part of the scheme, Rush used the program and fake government contract to trick an unnamed defense contractor into buying large amounts of gold that Rush then pocketed. According to The Post, the case astounded officials as Rush’s duties at the CIA involved work with one of the government’s “most sensitive intelligence-gathering programs,” with details so sensitive that only a few senior U.S. intelligence officials and lawmakers know of its existence. The Post withheld details of that program from publication as its disclosure could “jeopardize ongoing intelligence-gathering operations.” Court filings said that by virtue of his position, Rush “effectively acted as his own approving official, enabling him to expend substantial amounts of United States government money without meaningful scrutiny.” Rush is scheduled to be sentenced in late January 2027 and faces up to 20 years in prison. Meta rolls out new AI tools to detect ads that secretly lead to child sexual abuse material

Meta rolls out new AI tools to detect ads that secretly lead to child sexual abuse material

TechCrunch | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website

Meta announced Wednesday that it took action against 33.2 million pieces of child sexual exploitation content on Facebook and Instagram in the first half of 2026. At the same time, the company is rolling out new AI tools to find ads and accounts that may be used to secretly direct people to illegal child abuse material. More than 97% of the content Meta acted on was found by its systems before users reported it, the company said. In India, Meta acted on 5.3 million pieces of child sexual exploitation content during the same period, with more than 98% detected before users reported it. Meta said its latest AI tools are aimed at people using ads that look harmless to send users to illegal content outside Meta’s platforms. The company has introduced a new large language model (LLM) system to detect what it calls “signposting.” This refers to ads that may look normal but are suspected of directing users to illegal content or other harmful activity elsewhere online. Meta says this is a tactic it has recently seen bad actors use as they continue to change their methods to avoid detection. While the ads themselves may not contain illegal material, they can still act as a gateway to websites hosting child sexual abuse material. Because of this, Meta said it’s now looking at where an ad sends users, and not just what the ad contains. The company can use this information to block websites or other destinations that break its rules and take action against the accounts behind them. Meta is also using additional AI-driven scans to find child exploitation content that earlier systems may have missed. The company said it will continue adding new signals as it learns more about how these networks operate. Another new tool is a “red-teaming AI agent” that tests Meta’s own safety measures. It looks for weaknesses that bad actors could use to get around the company’s protections. Meta said this could help it find new methods of abuse before they become more common. Additionally, the company is improving its systems for finding people who return to its platforms with new accounts after their previous accounts have been removed. The new measures come as Meta continues to face pressure over the safety of children on its platforms. The company has faced lawsuits and criticism from lawmakers over the risks its social media services may pose to young users. In August, Meta reached an agreement to pay up to $18 billion to settle a child safety lawsuit involving 29 U.S. states, addressing concerns regarding the protection of children online. Meta has introduced several child-safety features this year, including parental controls for Meta AI, pre-teen accounts on WhatsApp, and alerts for parents when children search Instagram for self-harm content. In September, WhatsApp added more controls for parents. They can limit how teenagers use Channels, control who can see their status updates, and decide who can add their children to groups. Parents can also choose to receive notifications about certain group activity. When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. Healthleap raises $38M for its AI that flags hospital patients who may need a closer look

Healthleap raises $38M for its AI that flags hospital patients who may need a closer look

TechCrunch | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. Healthleap, a startup building an AI platform that reads patient records to identify patients at risk of undiagnosed illnesses, has raised $38 million in seed and Series A funding, TechCrunch has exclusively learned. The financing includes an $8 million seed round co-led by Sequoia Capital and First Round Capital, and a $30 million Series A led by Hummingbird Ventures. The company is not disclosing its valuation. Founded in South Africa in 2022 by siblings Jemima and Josiah Meyer (pictured above), the startup initially offered a clinical nutrition tool Jemima had built for dietitians. But the company later pivoted and built a more general-purpose platform that aims to identify patients admitted to hospitals who may be suffering from conditions like malnutrition or delirium that aren’t often identified early enough, CEO and co-founder Josiah Meyer told TechCrunch. “A patient’s chart holds two kinds of data. Labs, weights, and vital signs sit in structured fields, but the most telling signs sit in clinicians’ written notes: poor appetite, recent weight loss, muscle loss, trouble swallowing. Our developing approach is extracting affirmative or negated mentions of these clinical concepts in an easily extensible and scalable way,” Josiah said. The company’s platform is currently deployed in more than 50 hospitals, where it screens patients for conditions such as malnutrition and delirium, he said. The startup has also built programs to identify aspiration pneumonia, pressure ulcers, and risk of readmission for congestive heart failure, which Josiah said are undergoing further clinical validation. To find patients that may be at risk of an illness or were undiagnosed, Healthleap plugs into a hospital’s electronic health record system and uses language models to pull information from written notes, such as references to recent weight loss or difficulty swallowing. That analysis is then fed into its risk models, alongside structured information such as lab reports and vitals to surface patients who may need a closer look. The company notes that its software doesn’t diagnose patients, only highlights items for additional review. “Each night, we analyze every adult inpatient’s record: lab results, vital signs, weights, medications, diet orders, diagnoses, clinicians’ notes, and more,” Josiah explained. “Each morning, we write a risk score into the care team’s existing workflow with a dashboard accessible that holds additional information about the patients’ trends.” Malnutrition likely served as a useful starting point for Healthleap, as it’s a condition that often goes undiagnosed and can adversely impact patient recovery in a variety of ways. Research suggests 20% to 50% of hospital inpatients are malnourished, and some studies have linked malnutrition with longer stays, impaired wound healing, infections, and other complications, as well as higher morbidity and mortality. Josiah said Healthleap has grown from three hospital partners to more than 50 over the past year, and its customers now include Penn Medicine, Cedars-Sinai, Intermountain, Houston Methodist, and Emory Healthcare. Revenue has grown more than 10x over the same period, he said, though he didn’t disclose specifics. The startup sells three-year contracts priced according to a hospital’s licensed bed count, and also uses an outcome-based pricing model. “We use the hard ROI that the hospital finance team validates and attributes to us as the measurable ROI. Based on that, we contractually ensure that we deliver multiples of the contract price. To date, every customer has seen a 5x hard ROI or more, in some cases over 20x annual total ROI,” Josiah said. At the Hospital of the University of Pennsylvania, Healthleap says its malnutrition program resulted in $23.8 million in annualized financial impact, of which $6.3 million came from additional reimbursement and $17.5 million from shorter hospital stays. Healthleap plans to spend the fresh cash on engineering, product, sales, and customer success as it adds support for identifying more conditions. Meyer said the company ultimately wants to cover more than 40 major health conditions and expand into outpatient and home care. Bloom raises $3.6M to become the ‘Alibaba’ of American manufacturing

Bloom raises $3.6M to become the ‘Alibaba’ of American manufacturing

TechCrunch | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Last day to exhibit your breakthrough to 10,000+ tech leaders at Disrupt is on Oct 2. Book Exhibit Table Now. Disrupt doors open Oct. 13. Get your pass and bring someone with you at 50% off. REGISTER NOW. When Justin Kosmides co-founded Bloom in 2023, his goal was to create a savior for the mobility industry. He had seen many e-bike and e-scooter companies fail because of an inability — or an unwillingness — to outsource some of the hardest problems, like logistics, manufacturing, or building a supply chain. He wanted to convince survivors and new entrants to let Bloom handle that work for them. Then Donald Trump got re-elected and started shotgun-blasting tariffs on dozens of countries, partially to jump-start U.S. manufacturing. This accelerated a hardware scene that was already heating up in this country and, suddenly, it was more than just mobility companies looking to beef up their domestic supply chain. Robotics startups, drone-makers, and many more started to pop up. That was an opportunity for Bloom, which is based in Detroit, but to tackle it, the startup had to partially reinvent itself. Instead of performing some of those harder, behind-the-scenes tasks, which was a big part of the original plan, Bloom shifted to a pure marketplace model, in which it connects buyers and sellers. It’s now focused on making, essentially, supply chain AI agents that Bloom’s customers use to find specific types of suppliers, parts, or manufacturing and engineering services. The reinvention slowed Bloom’s fundraising plans. But the company has now made over 2,000 matches for more than 140 companies, and it wants to grow faster. In an interview earlier this year, Kosmides, who is Bloom’s CEO, said his startup is like what an AI-driven version of Alibaba did in China, where it created a marketplace for contract manufacturers. Investors are finally buying in. On Wednesday, Bloom announced a $3.6 million seed round led by SNAK Venture Partners, a marketplace-focused investment firm. Also joining were Flyover Capital (an early-stage investor that focuses on flyover states), and deep tech firm Mana Ventures. Local backers include Detroit Venture Partners, Invest Detroit Ventures, and the Michigan Outdoor Innovation Fund. Kosmides, sounding relieved, said in the interview that he’s “excited to be done with fundraising and get back to building.” SNAK first met with Bloom in April of last year, in the early stages of this reinvention. So early, in fact, that the firm passed on the chance to invest in the startup’s pre-seed round. “We liked the founder and the thesis and passed anyway: we wanted to see more traction,” the firm wrote in a blog post. “We said so plainly and kept tracking the company.” Bloom kept in touch with SNAK as it worked through becoming a more software-focused startup. By May of this year, Bloom had made as much revenue in five months as it did in all of 2025. SNAK also said the number of memberships on its platform had grown fivefold “with low churn,” meaning few customers were canceling. “This is meant to reiterate that for us, a pass on pre-seed is not always a pass forever. We are fortunate to have a super focused thesis that allows us to track a small set of interesting early stage companies and build a relationship over time,” the firm wrote. Kosmides said the fundraising scene has been a bit ruthless for startups like Bloom. Proving your value to investors in a world where the leading AI models keep improving can be an uphill battle. “To find investors to actually write a term sheet, not follow one, is getting harder and harder,” he said. “That’s just this new era that we’re in, with everyone trying to figure out what’s real and what’s not, what to invest in.” Bloom did court other term sheets, Kosmides said. But SNAK, which was founded by longtime retail executive Sonia Nagar, is “who you want in your camp” to “build a hopefully category-defining marketplace for mobility, and drones, and all this hardware.” Bloom’s platform handles both sides of a supplier relationship. Some customers use the platform to post contract opportunities, while others use it to bid on those opportunities. Bloom handles quoting, booking, and payment all on its platform, and it now helps match companies with suppliers that can do contract manufacturing, assembly, design and engineering, freight, warehousing, repairs, and even hazardous-materials shipping. “Our friends at the parts marketplaces like Fictiv, Xometry, MacroFab, and others are really good yesteryear marketplaces for finding one particular part,” Kosmides said. “But when you’re XYZ Drone Company, or an electric motorcycle company, and you need to find a provider that meets all these requirements, have them all bid on this opportunity, that’s a lot more complicated than finding a CNC part.” Making this system work well is a big part of Bloom’s reinvention, and it involved ingesting a lot of data about each provider on the platform. A lot of that was public data — how a company describes itself on its website, for instance. But around 30-40% of that data is coming directly from the companies, Kosmides said. Add in the data being generated as the startup onboards more customers and makes more matches, and you have Bloom’s edge, he said. “You can build the craziest model for scraping data but you would never get to that accuracy,” he said. “We started with manually booking services, and then built in layers and pieces and understanding as we built up the provider network, as we built out more and more transactions, and so it’s only getting better and better.” Kosmides believes this is all a better way to strike up supplier relationships, and that it also makes it easier for startups to get off the ground. But he also thinks Bloom’s platform will help create opportunities for small manufacturers who may not have big marketing budgets or sales teams. He gave an example of one Michigan-based contract manufacturer that, until it started working with Bloom, was taking on odd jobs like refurbishing Nest thermostats and Bird scooters, or working with Chick-fil-A displays. That same company is now bidding on drone assembly contracts. “The ability to discover, and the ability to do matchmaking, that is what I think is truly missing” from U.S. manufacturing, he said. “That just doesn’t exist electronically.” When you purchase through links in our articles, we may earn a small commission. SpaceX alumni nab $100M to rethink shipping with autonomous freight trains

SpaceX alumni nab $100M to rethink shipping with autonomous freight trains

TechCrunch | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website

REGISTER NOW. The railroad industry was one of the first to disrupt how the world worked with technology, but lately, innovation in freight railroading has amounted to little more than longer and longer trains. That has led many railroads to abandon shorter routes, ceding a chunk of the $1 trillion surface freight market to trucking companies. But as diesel prices reach record highs, some of those very trucking companies are going bust, leaving a hole in the market. Recently, at least 16 trucking companies went bankrupt over only a few weeks. Parallel Systems might have a way to keep freight moving while allowing railroads to recapture market share. The startup has developed a rail vehicle capable of moving over several tons of freight as far as 500 miles without an operator. If the company can scale production of its Panther vehicles, it stands to make railroads profitable over short hauls once again. “Less than 500 miles is hard for railroads to do competitively,” Matt Soule, co-founder and CEO of Parallel Systems, told TechCrunch. “Our technology allows them to take some of that trucking, and it’s for the public’s benefit.” Because it runs on railroads, Parallel’s vehicle doesn’t contribute to traffic congestion, and because it’s battery powered, it doesn’t pollute. To scale manufacturing of its third-generation vehicle and speed the commercial rollout, Parallel Systems has raised a $100 million Series C led by AVP, with participation from Hillspire, Agility Global, Cobalt Capital, Anthos Capital, Congruent Ventures, Riot Ventures, and Collaborative Fund. Soule and his co-founders started Parallel Systems in 2020 after years at SpaceX, where they designed rocket avionics systems. Parallel’s vehicles can travel either independently or in platoons, which tend to be shorter than typical trains. It’s an approach that bucks the prevailing strategy used by railroads, known as precision railroading: As the railroad industry has matured, investor pressure has driven companies to build longer trains and operate them on fixed schedules, which helps spread some costs like labor across more freight. As railroads have pulled back from smaller routes, trucking companies have stepped in. Today, some 60% of freight trips in the U.S. are under 500 miles, and trucking companies handle a majority of those. But more trucks on the road means more congestion, and nowhere is that more apparent than at ports. “If you ever go to the Port of Savannah, you see the truck lines coming in and out of the port — it’s insanity,” Soule said. Unlike traditional train cars, Parallel’s vehicles don’t have couplers, instead forming uncoupled platoons that move down the rails. The coupler-free design means that, once a platoon reaches a train yard, the vehicles can split up without needing human intervention. And after the Panther is cleared to operate on a section of track, its sensors monitor the rails to ensure the track ahead is clear of obstacles. Trucking companies have been intrigued by Parallel, despite the fact that the startup is claiming part of a business they currently own. Drayage companies, which move freight short distances, make money for each load they deliver, but they don’t get to charge more when a truck gets stuck in a traffic jam, even at a port where such congestion is common. By moving freight closer to a customer’s door, Parallel allows trucking companies to make more deliveries per day. “They want certainty, they want to be able to move,” Soule said. Parallel obtained approval a year and a half ago from the Federal Railroad Administration to operate near the Port of Savannah in Georgia, where it has been operating on 160 miles of track to prove the safety of its system. “We’ve been verifying all the safety controls and operating practices that are best evaluated in a real-world setting with a path toward our first commercial payload coming up very soon,” Soule said. What Worries Experts About the Russia 'Plague' Incident
2026-10-07

What Worries Experts About the Russia 'Plague' Incident

TIME | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The logo of Russia's consumer and health watchdog Rospotrebnadzor sits on its grounds' fencing in Moscow on Oct. 5, 2026. A laboratory worker in a plague research institute in Siberia died last week after developing pneumonia. Since then, local reports suggesting that she broke a test tube containing plague bacteria have fueled speculation about how she became ill. Russian authorities disputed that account and told the World Health Organization (WHO) that plague had not been detected in her tests nor in those of her close contacts. At the same time, Russian authorities have reportedly quarantined as many as 200 people, which public-health experts say would be an unusual response to an isolated case of pneumonia. On Tuesday evening, the WHO asked Russia for more information following media reports of a second case of pneumonia of unknown cause. Researchers who work in public-health policy and infectious diseases have been watching this rapidly evolving and opaque situation with concern. Here’s what worries them. The pattern of events doesn’t fit neatly with the explanation that a lab accident with a known bacterium sickened the worker, says Jennifer Nuzzo, director of the Pandemic Center at the Brown University School of Public Health. In labs that study Yersinia pestis, the bacterium that causes plague, protocols are in place to deal with accidental exposure. Wearing personal protective equipment and working under a fume hood provides some protection. But if someone finds they’ve been exposed, they would take antibiotics, which is an effective treatment for naturally occurring plaque bacteria. “You’d be fine. You don't die, typically,” says Nuzzo. A lab exposure “would be a regrettable accident that sometimes occurs.” That seems out of sync with the substantial response on the part of Russian authorities. “The magnitude and severity of that response is also worth looking at,” she says, referring to reports that up to 200 people are under observation and that local hospitals have been closed. “Now, that could just be bureaucratic reflexes. But I would feel more confident in what was going on if I knew specifically what she was tested for and what came back negative.” By this time, Nuzzo continues, “they should have a full sequence”—meaning the pathogen that killed the lab worker should be identified. Much is unknown about the situation, and some experts have said it’s worth considering the possibility that an engineered pathogen, rather than a naturally occurring one, could be involved. Dr. Ashish K. Jha, who coordinated the White House’s COVID response under President Joe Biden, wrote on X on Oct. 4 that “an accidental release from a deliberate program” was a scenario he had long worried about. Russia has a long history of bioengineering pathogens. After the fall of the Soviet Union, that nation’s programs were officially shuttered, but the current situation is unclear. “You can't help but worry that there is something more here than meets the eye,” Jha tells TIME. “What I will say from my conversations with friends in the [Trump] Administration is that they've been very worried about this…[But] they're not getting a lot of information and data.” ”I'm going to say something that's going to sound incredibly naive, but I'll say it: What I hope is that the Russians will be very forthcoming and transparent about what was going on in this lab, but one can imagine that they may not be,” Jha says. “If it's plague, breathe a sigh of relief, because we know what to do about plague,” says Nuzzo. “We have a clear solution…The worry is it's not that, it is something different, something for which we don't have easy solutions.” No, Nuzzo and Jha agree. We are in a worse situation now compared to pre-COVID times, Nuzzo says. “The protections we had in 2019, early 2020–there are now fewer of them, and we are more vulnerable now than we were then,” Nuzzo says, pointing to reduced monitoring and less information flow between countries. “This is why countries need to fund and maintain overseas scientific engagements, because then they know the actors, they know their motivations, they know what's normal. It's also why you want to have a strong, capable, and relied-upon WHO, because sometimes they're the best entity to have the conversations to surface the information.” Jha also flags the lack of monitoring: “The biggest weakness we have is we have almost no biodetection system in our own country.” If someone in Siberia was infected, got on a plane, and flew to New York City, “we would not know. We just need a much better global monitoring system on this.” Jha and a colleague from the Biden White House, Nikki Romanik, have started a public benefit corporation to build a monitoring system that draws on search and social media information, clinical information from hospitals, and wastewater sequencing. Wastewater monitoring typically looks for hand-picked pathogens, such as RSV or COVID-19. “Our strategy is you flip it, you sequence everything in the wastewater and you look for anything that doesn't belong. That's how we would detect it,” he says. The system, called BioRadar, is currently being tested in Dallas. It’s unlikely that the pathogen that caused the lab worker’s death—if it does turn out to be something new—would spread as effectively as COVID did, says Jha. Still, “a lot of the capabilities that I spearheaded building when I was at the White House for preparation for future outbreaks have been dismantled by U.S. Health and Human Services Secretary Robert F. Kennedy. A lot of stuff has been pulled back and taken away. We had put together a whole respiratory virus vaccine program called Project Next Gen that was taken down. We don't have mRNA vaccine platform funding anymore because of Secretary Kennedy. We are not nearly as ready as we need to be.” There is still the possibility that in the end, “this whole thing could be just a lot of coincidence and overreaction,” says Nuzzo. “And then people are going to be like, ‘You got us all whipped up about this thing.’ I am here to say, we have no idea. This could be nothing. But when your fire alarm goes off, you don't get mad at it for going off when nothing happened. You're glad nothing happened.” Ultimately, there could be real threats in our future, and the key is to be prepared, she says. Jha views the situation as a wake-up call to Americans. “Continue to pay attention, because that is important. Secondly, push our political leaders to get much more proactive. Because the truth is, you don't want to get concerned after the outbreak has happened. You want to be concerned before, and build the systems you need to make sure that we are ready for the next one,” he says. “Without sounding like an alarmist, look at what has happened in the last six months alone. There was the hantavirus outbreak on the cruise ship, and we're hearing from [AI company] Anthropic about all these people who are starting to use AI models to build biological weapons,” Jha says. “There's been this massive proliferation of high-risk labs around the world in the last five years, and so even if this one incident turns out to be not that big a deal, I am very worried we're going to see more and more things like this—and one of them will be a big deal.”

What to Say When Someone Shows Up Sick
2026-10-07

What to Say When Someone Shows Up Sick

TIME | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Your colleague has a fever, a packed calendar, and no intention of missing your 2 p.m. meeting. They make all of this clear as they plop down next to you in that stuffy conference room with a door someone has helpfully just closed. You’re less impressed by their work ethic than concerned about spending the next hour breathing the same air—yet saying something makes you feel like the rude one. Should you suggest they go home? Ask your boss to intervene? Subtly inch toward an open window? And how do you account for the possibility that they don’t feel they have a choice about being there? The same awkwardness can occur at home, where a friend arrives for dinner and mentions they’ve felt terrible all day—right after you hugged them hello. Asking them to leave can feel like delivering two uncomfortable messages: You did something wrong, and I don’t want you here, says Vanessa Bohns, a social psychologist at Cornell University and author of Should I Say Something?: The Science of What to Share—and What to Keep to Yourself. “If you say something to someone and you call them out, in a way you’re judging them,” she says. “It threatens their standing as a good person.” Suggesting they head home can also feel like rejecting them—especially if they showed up because they didn’t want to disappoint you. Still, Bohns says, “it’s worth saying something.” The key is to make your request clear while reassuring the person that you care about them and would like to see them when they’re feeling better. Here’s what to say when you’re facing a sick coworker, an ailing dinner guest, or some other ill person who insists they’re absolutely fine. Before deciding what to say, consider what you can realistically offer. Could you take notes for your colleague? Help them join remotely? Reschedule a meeting that depends on their participation? Giving someone a way to bow out with grace can make your request easier to accept, says Jodi R.R. Smith, an etiquette expert in Marblehead, Mass. “I’m going to take copious notes,” she suggests telling a colleague. Offer to send them afterward so they don’t feel they’ll miss out by heading home. How you phrase that suggestion depends partly on your role and relationship to them, adds Elaine Swann, an etiquette expert in Carlsbad, Calif. A manager can instruct someone to head home; a peer should frame it as a request. Either way, speak privately or send a quick message instead of calling someone out in front of the room. Dr. Heidi Torres, an infectious-disease physician and assistant professor of clinical medicine at Weill Cornell Medicine, makes it clear to her colleagues that the office can manage without them: “You stay home, you be guilt free. We are fine. We do not need you to be here.” If you’re the manager, make sure they know who will handle their workload after they leave. Someone who’s worried about an unfinished project may hear “go home” as “finish it from bed,” says Claire Smith, an occupational health psychologist at the University of South Florida who studies working while sick. Discuss what can wait, who can cover urgent tasks, and whether they’re expected to work at all. Working remotely removes the pressure to show up at the office, but it can leave people feeling they have no reason to take a sick day—even when they feel awful. Too often, Claire Smith says, employees have to decide whether they’re well enough to work and then figure out what to do about the meeting they’ll miss or the deadline they can’t meet. “It’s not a mutual conversation.” Of course, going home isn’t always as simple as grabbing your coat. Some people can’t afford to miss a shift; others worry they’ll be penalized for calling out. No carefully worded request can fix a lack of paid sick leave or a strict attendance policy, Claire Smith says. Before assuming someone is being inconsiderate, ask what’s keeping them there. If you can take something off their plate, be specific: “Would it help if I handled the client follow-up so you could head home?” That won’t replace a lost paycheck, but it might ease their worry about leaving work unfinished. What if the sick person is your boss? Bohns suggests framing your request around a shared work goal, especially if your manager treats pushing through illness as a badge of honor. “One of us should stay healthy for this project,” she suggests saying. You can then ask to join from your desk—making it clear that staying well is part of your commitment to the work. Ideally, conversations like these should start before anyone gets sick. Torres recommends establishing a coverage plan ahead of cold and flu season, so employees know who will take their shift or handle urgent work. Clear expectations also give coworkers something to point to when someone shows up unwell: You can remind them of the sick policy instead of making it a personal request. Managers need to follow those expectations themselves. Even a workplace that offers paid time off and backup coverage sends a conflicting message when the boss shows up with a fever. “That policy feels more like decoration,” Torres says. “They have to also follow that and lead by example.” Your friend may have dragged themselves to your dinner party because they thought you’d be disappointed if they canceled. Start by relieving that pressure, Bohns suggests: “I’m so sorry you’re feeling so lousy.” Then give them explicit permission to leave: “I hope you didn’t feel obligated to show up. You should definitely go home and rest.” You can soften the departure by packing up a container of dinner or sending them home with dessert. Before they go, make it clear you still want to see them: “We have to catch up. Let’s reschedule when you’re feeling better,” Bohns suggests. If they seem inclined to stay, be more direct. “I’m concerned about the rest of the guests,” Swann suggests saying privately. Ask them to call it an early evening, and resist announcing their departure to the group. “Allow the person to leave with dignity,” she says. If you’re a fellow guest, consider how well you know the person. A close friend might appreciate a whispered suggestion to head home and an offer to help arrange a ride, Jodi Smith says. If you barely know them, pull the host aside instead. Assume they’ve been busy serving dinner and greeting people and haven’t noticed what’s going on. Briefly explain your concern, then add: “I wanted to let you know so that you could handle it in the way that you see fit,” she suggests. Leave the decision to them; you can decide whether you’re comfortable staying. For your next gathering, Bohns suggests giving guests permission to cancel before anyone even has the sniffles. Tell people upfront that they shouldn’t feel pressure to attend if they’re unwell—and that you’ll invite them again. Someone who’s worried about seeming flaky may need to hear that staying home won’t cost them their place in the group. “It doesn’t mean you’re off the list,” she says. “You’re still going to be invited.” You’ve barely finished suggesting your friend or colleague head home when the reassurance begins: It’s just a cold. They feel fine. They’re definitely not contagious. Instead of arguing, Bohns suggests a reply that keeps the focus on you: “I’m just being extra careful right now.” Then explain what you’re going to do: “I hope you don’t mind if I stay over here.” Sharing a little context can help, too. Maybe you’re trying to stay healthy for a big upcoming trip, or you need to avoid bringing an illness home to a family member. Explaining that makes your request feel less like a judgment of their decision to show up, Bohns says. Those circumstances matter: What’s “just a cold” to one person could be a much bigger deal to someone else, Torres says. The person sitting nearby could be immunocompromised, take care of an elderly parent, or have a baby at home. At the same time, a cough doesn’t tell you everything. Torres says even she can’t always immediately distinguish allergies from an infection. If leaving isn’t an option, you can put on a mask or open a window to bring in fresh air—without needing to settle what’s making someone cough. If you’re ready to leave, you don’t have to wait for someone else to make the first move. Even if you’re the only person speaking up, you may not be the only one who’s uncomfortable. “We can see everyone else’s behavior, but we can’t see their feelings,” Bohns says. Your coworkers or fellow party guests might have the same concerns—and be waiting for someone else to say something. At work, Swann suggests privately messaging your manager or the meeting organizer to explain your concern and ask whether you can join remotely. That gives you a way to stay involved without spending the rest of the meeting next to someone who’s sick. At a party, you can simply thank the host and say you need to head out—even if they’re the one who’s under the weather. “Thanks for inviting me. I’ll catch up with you later,” Swann suggests. Keep your goodbye warm and brief, without announcing your reasons to everyone at the table. “If you don’t want to be around a sick person,” she says, “make the move yourself, and don’t try to get other people involved in it.”

The Real Whistleblower Behind The Social Reckoning and the Actor Who Played Her on Facebook, Mark Zuckerberg, and AI

The Real Whistleblower Behind The Social Reckoning and the Actor Who Played Her on Facebook, Mark Zuckerberg, and AI

TIME | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Frances Haugen and Mikey Madison during an interview with TIME. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The Social Network helped define a generation of tech founders when it premiered in 2010: Written by Aaron Sorkin, it tells the story of Facebook’s founding by an ambitious, jilted college kid who imposes his will on the world. Now, in The Social Reckoning, Sorkin focuses on the ills of the company Facebook eventually became. He tells the story through the lens of Frances Haugen, a former employee who demonstrated through a trove of internal documents, leaked to the Wall Street Journal, that Facebook’s algorithm was stoking political division, that Instagram was contributing to body-image issues for teen girls, and that the company’s platforms were being used by drug dealers and human traffickers. CEO Mark Zuckerberg has denied his company, rebranded as Meta after Haugen’s leak, prioritized profit over safety. Mikey Madison, fresh off her Oscar for Anora, plays Haugen in the film. Madison and Haugen sat with TIME for their first interview together. Madison:  When I got the script, I knew about the Facebook Files, but I didn’t know Frances’ name. So I looked her up, and I was like, “She’s blonde and has blue eyes. Why does Aaron want me for this?” And he said, “I want you to try and build the character from my script.” But of course I read her memoir and tried to do my due diligence because there was so much I didn’t understand. I don’t have a personal relationship with social media at all. Madison: It’s just something that never quite stuck. It’s something I never felt I could understand. When I was 13, I had an Instagram, but it kind of lived and died at that time and not for any particular reason or self-preservation. Haugen: I first met Aaron a few months after I came forward as a whistleblower, and he wanted to figure out if it was possible to do a companion piece to The Social Network. After my memoir came out, he came back to me with this heavily underlined, sticky-noted, dog-eared copy. Madison: I don’t know if much can prepare you for that. I’m not someone who naturally speaks in that rhythmic way. I don’t know anyone who does, except for maybe Aaron. It’s singular to his sensibilities as a screenwriter. I keep joking that Aaron has this internal stopwatch in his head. Every period, every comma is incredibly intentional, and the space between is timed, and we’re on a deadline. The first day on set, after seven or eight takes, he came in and was like, ‘OK, we’re starting to cook with a bit of gas here.’ Madison: But I also think it created a certain intensity. Haugen: There’s a fidelity to it. [Wall Street Journal reporter] Jeff [Horwitz] and I are both chatty people who talk quickly, so you do capture the excitement of when we were in the weeds. Jeremy Allen White as Jeff Horwitz and Mikey Madison as Frances Haugen Courtesy of Sony Haugen: We’re only in the first chapter of the settlements. We still have two major sets of settlements to go just in the United States. The school districts are bringing very large suits, which I’m expecting to have dollar values north of $100 billion, maybe $200 billion. And then we have the individual kids and family lawsuits. So far, we’ve only had one resolve, but it was $6 million to a single person. When you apply that across kids in the United States, you only need a fraction of 1% [of that amount], and it adds up to a trillion dollars. Haugen: Mark is in a unique psychological circumstance in that there are very few founders in Silicon Valley who have the level of control he has at the scale he has...I think he’s just been isolated for so long it’s distorted his priorities. Madison: I had less than half a day to work with him. We had one scene, but I was almost a background actor. I hope to be able to work with him in the future. His process is intriguing to me as an actress. The result is obviously very special. Frances Haugen and Mikey Madison attend 'The Social Reckoning' world premiere on Oct. 04, 2026 in New York City Dimitrios Kambouris—Getty Images Haugen: I think you can square the circle on capitalism and what is good for us. Five years ago, people in the United States used Instagram and Facebook more than they do today. And part of that is because Meta over-optimized for the short term instead of the long term. When you see unpleasant content, when you see AI slop and bullying and more ads, you’re less likely to use the product. Governance helps businesses make choices that optimize for the long term, which actually makes them more profitable long-term. So capitalism doesn’t have to be in conflict with safety. When it comes to AI, part of what makes it hard is the rate of change. I’m hopeful we’re going to have one or two more scary incidents, and then we’re going to be like, “Maybe we should do something sensible.” Haugen:  I went from someone who could ride 125 miles a day on a bike to being in a wheelchair in two and a half years. One day my leg turned blue and it turned out that I had a foot-long clot the girth of a cigar in my leg, and they had to do surgery multiple times. Unfortunately while I was in the hospital I had an allergic reaction to the blood thinner. During that time, I went through my worst fears. I lost my job because they thought I was faking being sick. My husband left me. I lost my health. And I came back from it. So I do feel that phase of my life changed my ability to take risks. In terms of the movie, Aaron Sorkin did a miraculous thing: He made typing at a computer compelling for two hours. But you have to make choices. I think he chose to focus on the things that would give us the most insight into not just my experience but how Facebook and Mark work. Haugen:  Do you think Mark is going to watch the movie? Madison: I do. How do you feel about that? Haugen: My hope is that he watches the movie and sees Jeremy Strong’s performance and asks himself, “Do I want to be living this life? Is this the legacy that I want?” Because sometimes you can’t see it in the mirror until art reflects it.

How Oct. 7 Changed Israel
2026-10-07

How Oct. 7 Changed Israel

TIME | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The Nova music festival memorial site on Oct. 07, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The world changed when Hamas attacked Israelis on Oct. 7, 2023, catalyzing devastating conflict throughout the region. But within Israel, political sentiments have changed surprisingly little. To some, the attack fulfilled long-term right-wing warnings that Israel’s withdrawal from Gaza would yield disaster; and the attack was incontrovertible proof that Palestinians will never live in peace with Israel. Israeli left-wingers, for their part, have long held that a festering military rule over Palestinians in the West Bank and Gaza, and failure to reach peace, would inevitably lead to violent conflagration. For them, Oct. 7 only proved that they had been right all along. The standoff raises crucial questions. In three years since Oct. 7, how has the attack changed Israeli society? And how will the answer affect upcoming elections in Israel? To be sure, there have been cataclysmic changes in Israel, and the Middle East at large, since Oct. 7. The war in Gaza that began that day has become Israel’s longest war. A relatively stable ceasefire with Lebanon from 2006 to 2023 ended when Hezbollah, a Shi’a militia group, fired on Israel the day after Hamas’ attack; after multiple escalations since then, the current ceasefire is precarious. Israel and the U.S. have waged two full-blown wars with Iran, and the Middle East is still threatened by spin-off conflicts. The losses Israelis suffered on Oct. 7 and since then have affected the country collectively and profoundly. For two years, many Israelis went from demonstration to demonstration demanding that their government prioritize a deal with Hamas to release Israeli hostages. Hundreds of thousands have spent the last three years in and out of active reserve duty. Since 2023, Israel has seen record levels of emigration and growing international isolation. Sixty-one percent of Israelis told pollsters for the Israel Democracy Institute (IDI) that the national mood is poor. Moreover, the destruction of Palestinian life and society, still ongoing, will one day force a terrible reckoning within Israel. What’s less visible, and more surprising, is what has stayed the same. Israelis’ attitudes towards Palestinians, occupation, and conflict have become more extreme. But I would argue that Israeli public opinion today says much more about how Israel had changed prior to Oct. 7. The change in national attitudes since that fateful day has been moderate and consistent with what came before. In December 2022, almost a year before Oct. 7, support for peace through a two-state solution hit a historic low. In the joint Palestinian-Israeli survey I conduct together with colleagues at Tel Aviv University and the Palestinian Center for Policy and Survey Research, support stood at 39% of Israelis that year—the lowest since the 1990s. Among Jewish respondents, just 34% supported a two-state solution, and 37% believed Israel should permanently annex the West Bank while Palestinians would have limited rights. When we conducted the study again in mid-2024, Jews were twice as likely to support a single, Israeli-dominated unequal state as they were to support two states. Palestinian attitudes in 2022 were similar. Just one-third supported two states. Both sides showed a mirror-image majority who distrusted the other. In surveys by the IDI from that same time, 59% of Jewish Israelis defined themselves as right-wing; just 12% said they were left-wing. What changed after Oct. 7? This July, the Tel Aviv University Peace Index survey found that support for two states stood at 31%, an eight-point decline in the four years since 2022. Now, IDI surveys find that just over 60% of Israeli Jews describe themselves as right-wing, just a few points higher than late 2022. Israel has changed, but this change began years before Oct. 7 2023. In 2010, no fewer than 71% of Israelis supported a two-state solution in the joint surveys. The years that followed were plagued by stagnation and dissipation of any peace process, regular wars with Hamas, and a heavily nationalist government in Israel. It stands to reason that these phenomena pushed Israeli society further to the right. Support for the two-state solution eroded slowly, then rapidly by the end of the decade. That shift was self-reinforcing: the more right-wing Israelis became (primarily the Jewish population), the more elected leaders took ever-more radical positions such as supporting settlement expansion and de facto annexation in the West Bank. In 2019, Prime Minister Netanyahu began referring to extending sovereignty over parts of the West Bank, or de jure annexation. The current government accelerated those processes drastically. Study after study shows that Arab citizens of Israel have remained the strongest supporters of peace, before and after the current wars. But with Jewish Israeli and Palestinian attitudes so dismal in the years before Oct. 7, the chances of resurgent violence almost seemed inevitable. In other words, most Israelis did not radically transform their worldview after Oct. 7. In fact, hardline political trends were among the many factors contributing to the terrible escalation to begin with. Another thing that hasn’t changed in Israel during the last three years of war: hopes for peace. The number of Israeli Jews who self-describe as left-wing has barely shifted. In a May survey for the Institute for National Security Studies, about 20% of the total population, Jews and Arabs, define themselves as left-wing, and a similar share typically say they are centrist. These groups embrace a commitment to democracy, liberal values, separation of religion and state, and reject the religious fundamentalism of the current government. The left remains committed to peace via diplomacy and Palestinian self-determination. In a mid-2025 survey I conducted for a peace organization called A Land for All, where I am also an active member, the large majority of self-defined left-wing Israelis, and nearly half of centrists, supported a basic two-state solution. Do peace attitudes even matter, when there is no peace process? That may be theoretical for now, but elections aren’t. Israelis will be voting on Oct. 27. Polls show the voters deeply divided, just as they were in the elections held between 2019 and 2022. Netanyahu’s far-right coalition is not doing well. Nearly all polls show his coalition parties well below their actual result in 2022. And most mainstream polls show a consistent majority for today’s opposition parties. At first glance, these shifts might seem antithetical. Aren’t these ideological and electoral trends moving in contradictory directions: more right-wing, but a majority against the current government? It turns out that even the Israeli electoral contradiction is not new. During the five elections from 2019 through 2022, a majority of Israelis were right-wing and voted for right-wing parties. But to form a coalition, some of those parties no longer supported Netanyahu and refused to join a coalition under his leadership. Netanyahu therefore commanded only about half the seats in parliament following four of these cycles. Neither side had a clear or firm coalition majority, and the country was thrown into electoral turmoil five times over. But if all mainstream polls show the anti-Netanyahu parties winning, including right-wing opposition parties, shouldn’t that mean a decisive outcome against him this time? Not quite: no poll shows Israel’s Jewish, Zionist parties reaching a majority in October. And in Israel’s right-wing, populist discourse, the main opposition party leaders opposed to Netanyahu now, Naftali Bennett and Avigdor Lieberman, with similar statements from frontrunner Gadi Eisenkot, insist that parties representing Arab citizens must not be part of a coalition after Oct. 7. Together they will likely hold a critical number of parliamentary seats. It’s a view that implicitly (and falsely) connects them with the Hamas attacks. Those Arab or Palestinian citizens of Israel have been stalwart supporters of peace, and were among the victims of Oct. 7. A large majority, nearly three-quarters of them, want an Arab party to join Israel’s governing coalition, according to polling by the Moshe Dayan Center at Tel Aviv University. But if Israeli party leaders ultimately prioritize their ideological convictions (or political posturing) against Arabs over their rejection of Netanyahu, their options will be limited since they look unlikely to win a majority of Knesset seats in October. Netanyahu has much working against him: ongoing corruption trials, the fact that he was the leader who presided over the terrible failures of Israel to prevent Oct. 7, three years of wars, and the unconscionable destruction of Gaza. Yet he could still form the next government if the opposition can’t do it—and lead it. In that case, the same conditions in place on Oct. 6 2023, such as Israeli annexation of Palestinian areas, now with aspirations for Gaza as well as the West Bank, suppression of Palestinian self-determination, ongoing occupation and wars with no end in sight, will continue. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

El Niño Is Bringing Kelvin Waves to California. Here's What To Know
2026-10-07

El Niño Is Bringing Kelvin Waves to California. Here's What To Know

TIME | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Homes off the Alamitos Peninsula braces for waves from a waning swell from Hurricane Marie combined with a 6 ft. high tide that has been flooding streets and homes on Sept. 8, 2026 in Long Beach, Calif. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings A large pulse of wave energy generated by El Niño conditions, known as a Kelvin wave, is expected to travel up the West Coast, raising sea levels and increasing the risk of coastal erosion for beachfront towns. As a result, the West Coast can expect to see sea levels begin to rise by up to 1 ft. this month—and scientists say that the phenomenon can create dangerous conditions for humans, and place deadly stress on marine wildlife as it travels up the coastline. Read more: Why California Just Declared a State of Emergency Due to El Niño Kelvin waves are named after the Scottish engineer, mathematician, and physicist who identified the phenomenon, William Thomson, also known as Lord Kelvin. They are not like the typical waves you see crashing into the beach. They’re planetary waves—large, slow-moving masses of water that can span thousands of miles. Warm water slowly moves along the equator, towards South, Central and North America and can raise sea level for months. On the surface, their impact might not be particularly noticeable. “It pushes the warm water down deeper and produces a ridge in the ocean itself,” says Paul Roundy, professor of atmospheric science at University at Albany. That causes sea levels to rise, he says. “You would not see anything different, but the sea level would be just a few centimeters higher than it would normally be.” While it might not sound like much, coupled with other factors, it can have a major impact on coastlines. “[If] you add that sea level change to the normal tides or strong tide, and then potentially add a storm on top of it, which El Niño intensifies, then it can start to have a meaningful effect on changing the beach erosion and flooding at the coast,” says Roundy. This season has already brought a few Kelvin waves to the West Coast. “We've already had probably three or four of them up along the west coast of North America so far,” says Roundy. A number of coastal California towns, including Malibu, Laguna Beach, and Long Beach, have already seen significant coastal erosion this year—due to a number of factors, including El Niño and Pacific hurricanes. At the end of September, California Governor Gavin Newsom declared a state of emergency to help prepare the state for potential El Niño impacts. The last strong El Niño event, which took place from 2015-2016, had five Kelvin waves. On the other hand, the 2022-2023 El Niño, did not produce strong Kelvin waves. That’s because a number of other weather events—including weak westerly winds—did not produce the kind of conditions meant to form them. Kelvin waves commonly occur during El Niño years, when trade winds that normally push warm surface water toward the western Pacific weaken or change directions, moving warm water east and initiating a Kelvin wave. There’s a lot of scientific debate about how climate change is impacting El Niño events. However, global temperatures trend higher during El Niño years, as warm water adds heat into the atmosphere. That, coupled with already record breaking global temperatures due to human caused climate change, has scientists predicting that  2027 is likely to be the world's hottest year on record.

Artificial Is a Hugely Entertaining Movie About Despair for the Future
2026-10-07

Artificial Is a Hugely Entertaining Movie About Despair for the Future

TIME | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Andrew Garfield as Sam Altman and Yura Borisov as Ilya Sutskever in Artificial Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings If you’re like most people, your preoccupations in late summer 2026 were fairly workaday: Maybe you were negotiating the eternal challenge of the life-work balance, or fretting about escalating prices for gas and groceries. Then, in early September, former Anthropic researcher Jacob Coxon blew a whistle to drown out all other whistles: He warned that, given the current irresponsibility of the companies in charge, AI could easily spin out of control and “kill us all by the end of the decade.” If imminent human extinction—as opposed to the more slow-burning decline posed by global warming—hadn’t been on your list of worries, you now had a reason to add it. Luca Guadagnino’s tragicomedy Artificial—which premiered at the New York Film Festival on Oct. 5 and opens in limited release on Dec. 25, provided the world hasn’t ended by then—lays out with alarming clarity how we got here, and offers only the most threadbare scrap of hope that we might avert disaster. Strangely, though, Artificial isn’t a total downer; it’s essentially about human ideals gone off the rails, a reflection on how wrong things can go when brilliant, optimistic scientists fail to grasp the real danger of the thing they’ve set in motion. Artificial opens in 2003, in the University of Toronto lab of Geoffrey Hinton, the man who’d come to be known as the godfather of AI, and ends with a postscript from Hinton about the necessity of curtailing the capabilities of AI, fast. Yet beneath its potentially oppressive, ominous layers, it's a movie filled with life, and in places, it’s laugh-out-loud funny. Ike Barinholtz, of The Studio, practically brings the house down as a circa-2015 Elon Musk, a guy who seems to shamble through life looking only vaguely, comically zonked, not dangerously so. If nothing else, Artificial shows us how naïve we all were not 50 years ago, or even 20; the world is spinning so fast now that a decade goes by in the blink of an eye. Musk is a minor player in Artificial, almost a curlicue attached to the bigger story. But not even OpenAI CEO Sam Altman—played here, with unnerving malevolence by Andrew Garfield—is the movie’s star character. Artificial really belongs to OpenAI cofounder Ilya Sutskever (Yura Borisov), whom we first meet as a brash 17-year-old kid enrolled in the University of Toronto. He’s the one who first, shyly, knocks on the door of computer scientist, cognitive scientist, cognitive psychologist, and all-around genius Hinton (Mark Rylance, his eyebrows two sincere, somber, science-brain hyphens), hoping to be taken under his wing. Hinton isn’t immediately impressed. Ilya is quiet, lumbering, extremely awkward. But if his English is sluggish, his mind is quick as a lightning strike, and he has high hopes for the ways artificial intelligence might help mankind. His ideas align with Hinton’s, and by 2013 the two of them—along with a third partner, Fedor Fedotov’s Alex Krizhevsky—find themselves at Google, innocently pursuing their research. When Ilia first meets Altman, there’s not even the faintest clang of an alarm bell. At that point, Altman, as portrayed by Garfield, is just a skinny, eccentric and possibly vaguely charming guy who’d just happened to make a pile of dough off a tech startup he’d founded, which he later parlayed into various lucrative investments. With Musk and others, he’d already started OpenAI, a nonprofit at the time, with the intent of keeping the development of AI technology in the hands of the people. It’s 2015, and he invites Ilya to a private event in Menlo Park called, somewhat ominously, A Dinner for the Future. The dinner is really just a noisy roundtable of badly dressed tech whizzes drinking shots and shoveling expensive food into their gobs. But somehow, in the midst of all this frat-boy revelry, Ilya is able to outline his vision for the future, one in which AI will have solved numerous human problems, including eradicating not only cancer but also loneliness and unhappiness. By now we can see that Altman, the man presiding over the table, is a wily, evil elf. Somehow, though, Ilya trusts him and leaves Hinton and Google for OpenAI, and the chain of sterling intentions that he’s taken such care in forging begins to tarnish. Artificial is a story of unchained ambitions and betrayals, with its Altman an unqualified villain. Guadagnino, screenwriter Simon Rich, and Garfield have fashioned him as a pure sociopath, a conniving agent of chaos with Mr. Spock eyebrows and dark eyes that glint like a serpent’s. As Garfield plays him, Altman makes a practice of manipulating those around him for his own ends, though he’ll also run to his boyfriend, mewling with self-pity, when he feels he’s been wronged. (Guadagnino seems to have no compunction about fashioning Altman as an “evil gay”; sometimes you’ve just got to call it as you see it.) Garfield's performance is both hilarious and truly unsettling. His Altman—sucking down his green smoothies, pouting when things don’t go his way, visiting his neon-lit Napa Valley bunker filled with automatic weapons—is a big baby man who, along with a small handful of others, holds the fate of humanity in his hands. This is the way the world ends, not with a bang but a tech bro. Artificial features a large, sprawling cast, including Cooper Hoffman as one of Altman’s original OpenAI cronies, and an underused Monica Barbaro as Mira Murati, the Tesla product manager who became one of OpenAI’s key players. But Borisov is the movie's key. The Ilya of Artificial is a fumbling genius who, unlike his tech compatriots, pads around in sandals instead of big, ungainly sneakers, who sits down at the keyboard when he’s feeling tense, who stumbles with shyness when he's first confronted with Murati's brains and beauty. Borisov, who broke through as a thug with a poet’s soul in the 2024 Anora, vests Ilya with the same spiritual contours. You could argue that Artificial is a movie of stark bad guys and good ones, with no middle ground. But maybe, here, there is no middle ground. Ilya’s principled belief in the ways AI might have been used to help mankind is the sort of thing to which you can’t attach a price tag. You can’t say the same about ChatGPT which, as the movie tells it, was rushed to market before anyone had bothered to check its tendency to “hallucinate,” let alone furnish users with dangerous or harmful information. If Artificial is a movie you think you have no interest in seeing, think again—if only because the people developing, and profiting off, AI technology will surely hope you won’t see it. Guadagnino developed the movie at Amazon MGM, which ultimately declined to release it, not least because Amazon had already announced its own multi-billion-dollar investment in OpenAI. (The film will now be distributed by Neon.) But it’s important to know that you needn’t approach Artificial with feelings of dread. What makes the movie distinctive is how enjoyably watchable it is. Shot by Malik Hassan Sayeed, it has a vital, vibrant look; its vibe is more teen comedy than Important Picture of Our Time. This isn’t an exceedingly noble, dutifully made picture like Aaron Sorkin’s The Social Reckoning, in which another Anora alum, Mikey Madison, plays a Facebook employee who blows the whistle on that company’s eagerness to court extremist groups as a way of driving up engagement. The Social Reckoning is hardly a terrible film, but it doesn’t have the verve and energy that Artificial does. Guadagnino has made an exhilarating movie that’s also ultimately despairing: Though he’s done a great job of laying out the story of how we got here, he can’t even pretend there’s an easy solution. Yet Artificial doesn’t come off as a helpless shrug. It’s too muscular a piece of filmmaking for that. If we really are being led to our doom by a small handful of greedy, power-mad players, let’s not go down without a fight. Let’s be like Ilya at the keyboard, picking out a tune even in a moment of duress. In the face of an almost incomprehensibly inhuman threat, being the ultimate human is the only answer, from our first breath to our last.

"Synchrotron light sources for drug discovery?" This does a nice job making it clear using concrete example that basic or foundational science, though pursued for the sake of knowledge (or high-confidence information at the least), often without an immediately obvious application or practical upshot, often leads through winding and indirect paths to incredibly important practical discoveries. No basic science would mean no technical progress after we work out the consequences of what we already know. Even though some regions or countries stay on a shifted time offset year-round (which might look or feel like being "ahead" like daylight saving time), standard time is legally and geographically considered the baseline time zone for those areas. Countries & Regions That Do Not Change ClocksMost of Asia: This includes major industrialized nations like China, India, Japan, and South Korea, as well as most of Southeast and Central Asia. Most of Africa: Almost the entire African continent stays on a single standard time year-round. Parts of the Middle East: While countries like Iran and formerly parts of the Levant have experimented with it, places like Turkey and several neighbors maintain fixed times. Parts of Europe: Iceland, Belarus, Russia, Georgia, Armenia, and Azerbaijan do not practice seasonal time changes. (The European Union has debated abolishing the practice entirely, but member states still currently shift together). Much of South and Central America: Countries like Argentina, Brazil (which abandoned nationwide DST a few years ago), and most of their neighbors remain on permanent standard time. Every time I read that a company is "Aligning" the only thing I can assume that is being aligned is the C-suite's pocket books. The fact that the companies doing this the most seem to be the ones with the biggest revenues is a pretty good clue this is only about increasing stock dividends (and thus C-Suite bonuses) "laid-off employees will receive 20 weeks of base pay, one week per year of service (capped at 30 weeks), five months of COBRA and Modern Health healthcare benefits, and they will be allowed to keep their laptops." Most small tech companies gave much less than that to employees affected by a layoff. Heck, after 27 years at one company all I got was my back pay, and vacation pay when the reaper finally came for me. The company was on the decline so I guess that was to be expected. Had to live off savings for a while. The reason they are generous is to keep other employees from being spooked. If you think more layoffs are coming (it's always the case more layoffs are coming) and you are afraid of being unemployed with no job prospects, you'll start looking for a job now. And when the brain drain starts it's always the people the company least wants to lose who walk first. HubSpot appears to be one of the worst Email spam entites in the world. I block them completely. Everything that is being done now is breaking old assumptions about good will of everyone involved. People deserve to be payed for their work. People making things deserve respect. A Progressive Democrat Tried to Woo MAHA Republicans. The Early Response Is Revealing.
2026-10-07

A Progressive Democrat Tried to Woo MAHA Republicans. The Early Response Is Revealing.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Locked in a tight Senate race in Michigan, progressive Democrat Abdul El-Sayed is vying for every vote he can get—and even some he probably can’t. El-Sayed is reaching out to the “Make America Healthy Again” movement, a loosely affiliated group of voters, many of them part of Donald Trump’s 2024 coalition, best known for their opposition to vaccines, as well as their concern about chemicals in our food, water, and air. In a bid for their support, the Michigan Democrat is pushing what his campaign calls the “Building a Healthier America” agenda, which focuses on having fewer chemicals and toxins in food and crops, healthier lunches, more funding for public health programs, breaking up “corporate concentration in agriculture,” and purging Michigan’s drinking water of microplastics. The agenda is big on healthier lunches in schools, and the campaign is calling for public policy to “integrate healthy food into how Michigan delivers healthcare.” El-Sayed’s effort is representative of a broader push among Democrats to make inroads into a MAHA movement that Trump largely captured by co-opting its standard-bearer. The movement’s unofficial leader is Robert F. Kennedy Jr., who ran for president in 2024 before dropping out and endorsing Trump. Not coincidentally, Trump subsequently picked Kennedy to be his Health and Human Services secretary. Atop HHS, Kennedy has taken a much more adversarial stance toward vaccines and vaccine requirements, a reflection of his longtime push to connect vaccines for children with health issues, including autism. (To be clear, Kennedy is trafficking in conspiracy theories and is on the wrong side of science. Vaccines are safe and effective in preventing diseases that once made child mortality catastrophically higher than it is today.) But the MAHA-MAGA coalition is not without its points of friction, particularly when it comes to agriculture and pesticide companies with deep pockets and close ties to lawmakers in both parties—but particularly farm-state Republicans. MAHA had hoped for more restrictive policies on pesticides and a crackdown on industrialized agriculture, but they’ve come away disappointed. In February, Trump signed an executive order promoting the production of a weed-killing chemical that MAHA had targeted, leaving many in the movement disillusioned. They were further disappointed when Kennedy came out in support of the order in one of the more obvious fall-on-your-pesticide-laden-sword moments of recent political history. Democrats are hoping to exploit that rift. “It’s smart for Abdul to appeal to MAHA voters in Michigan, some of whom are definitely persuadable given President Trump’s record on what he told MAHA he would do in office versus what he’s done,” a veteran Michigan political strategist said. Nabbing even a few MAHA voters could go a long way. El-Sayed is running against Republican Mike Rogers, and most recent polls put the Democrat narrowly ahead. There’s some evidence of crossover appeal. “We are very interested in any candidate who is taking the issue of pesticides, toxins in our environment, and health seriously,” said Zen Honeycutt, who identifies as part of the MAHA movement and is the founding executive director of Moms Across America, a prominent group within the broader MAHA movement. There is an enormous sticking point to all of this: vaccines—and, more specifically, vaccine mandates. “I understand that [El-Sayed] believes in vaccines, and that’s fine with most of us as long as he doesn’t push his beliefs on us,” Honeycutt said. “Meaning, as long as, as an elected official, he does not support vaccine mandates that prevent our children from going to school or camp or church or anything of the like.” I asked El-Sayed’s campaign where the candidate stands. The campaign pointed to El-Sayed telling CBS that he would “always be evidence-driven when it comes to vaccines … but the key thing is that you want to know what you’re feeding your kids and what you’re putting in the body is pure. And RFK Jr. and Donald Trump and Mike Rogers have failed that in droves.” El-Sayed added that he looked forward to “listening and learning” and that “where there might be disagreements on vaccine policy, I think we all agree that Big Pharma is a real problem.” That’s a notable nonanswer on whether he supports vaccine mandates, which are in place in the overwhelmingly majority of schools nationwide. (It turns out that most parents don’t want their kids sharing toys with peers who are, due to their parents’ choices, at elevated risk for carrying deadly diseases.) Basically, the candidate is saying that he’ll hear MAHA out on vaccines, but his policy preferences on the issue aren’t going to align with theirs. And for Honeycutt and her fellow ideological travelers, that sure seems like a dealbreaker. “I guarantee you our moms will be looking into his stance on vaccines,” she said of El-Sayed. “If he at all hints at being in favor of vaccine mandates, that will cancel out his appeal as far as pesticides and making America healthy again.” MAHA is an expansive movement, and Honeycutt doesn’t speak for all of it. But it’s difficult to imagine many MAHA voters abandoning their signature issue for an El-Sayed vote. That’s not a dismissal of the outreach attempt so much as a reflection of where the MAHA movement is in reality. Democrats would have a decent shot at voters who equally weighted vaccines with, say, organic food or clean-water protections, but that voter is more often aspirational than corporeal. Because on vaccines, the issue on which MAHA voters are most decisively in conflict with the scientific consensus, they are the most dug in. And no number of white papers, roundtables, or outreach events is going to change that. The bulk of El-Sayed’s efforts, however, still focus on turning out Democrats’ base. On Monday, the campaign said that later this month the Senate contender would be campaigning alongside former President Barack Obama. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism.
