Christa Pike’s Botched Execution Was Even More Gruesome Than We’ve Been Led to Believe
2026-10-06

Christa Pike’s Botched Execution Was Even More Gruesome Than We’ve Been Led to Believe

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-06

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Last week, six members of the United States Supreme Court blessed a cruel and unusual horror show at Riverbend Maximum Security Institution in Nashville, Tennessee. In lifting a stay and greenlighting the state of Tennessee to execute Christa Pike by lethal injection, SCOTUS set in train a series of grotesque but predictable events. Not only did Tennessee fail to kill Christa Pike, but they then had to rush her to a hospital for life-saving care. Pike’s legal team had warned that this method of execution would be “difficult and perhaps cruel” due to a blood condition and small veins. Pike is currently in critical condition on a ventilator, with burned and blistered arms, according to her lawyers. On this week’s Amicus podcast, Dahlia Lithwick spoke with legal scholar Corinna Barrett Lain, author of Secrets of the Killing State: The Untold Story of Lethal Injection, about the many ways Pike’s botched execution ripped the mask off America’s preferred method of medicalized capital punishment. Their conversation has been edited and condensed for clarity. Dahlia Lithwick: In a country that historically did beheadings, hangings, all sorts of objectively gross executions, the decision was made somewhere along the line to medicalize executions, to make it look like dental surgery. I think you’ve likened it to putting down a dog. It has been sanitized, making it easier to believe we’re not actually torturing a person. Can you talk about that impulse to make it look medical, and all the obfuscation that comes along with it, and how this attempted execution really blew that up? Corinna Barrett Lain: So when you think of the obfuscation, you know, you might want to ask yourself: Why are they on a gurney? Why do they have white sheets over them? Why are the people inserting the catheter wearing white coats? They’re not doctors. Why are they wearing white coats? Why do we swab the arm with alcohol before injecting the drugs to kill them? It’s ridiculous. It’s all stagecraft. It’s all for us. And when it goes wrong, as it did in Christa Pike’s attempted execution, they lower the blinds. It’s a statement that they lowered the blinds twice, and then they told the media to leave. The media could still hear her back there. So the media knew she was still alive, struggling, in the midst of her execution, and yet they made the media leave. With medicalization, we assume care, we assume competence, we certainly assume humanity. What my research has shown is that lethal injection, from the start, wasn’t so much about a humane execution as a humane-looking execution. I think an important data point is that a federal court at the time had recognized a First Amendment right to televise executions. It wasn’t to last, but nobody could have guessed that. So you had legislators in Texas that talked about that ruling and said, “We can’t have an electrocution in someone’s living room.” Others said, “It can’t look like an execution room. It can’t look like a prison. I can think of a way that this would have a very humane vibe if it was more of a medical setting.” They were very much talking about how lethal injection looked. I have come to think that lethal injection has done a tremendous amount of work in maintaining the death penalty because it’s violence, but it’s all under a chemical veneer—there’s nothing to see here, move along, out of sight, out of mind. The idea was for the public not to be thinking about state violence, not to be thinking we’re actually killing people on purpose, indeed not to be thinking about the death penalty at all. That did blow up with Christa Pike. One of the most humiliating moments for the state of Tennessee—and we’ve never seen this in the modern era—is for them not only to fail (there’ve been other executions that are so botched that the person survives their own execution), but here, not only did they fail, but then they had to bring her to the hospital for life-saving care. The very person that you were trying to kill. It’s just an epic disaster on so many fronts. We don’t actually know why she survived her own execution. The state of Tennessee knows. We don’t know. That’s a problem in itself. I have some pretty good educated guesses about what happened. One possibility, a huge possibility, is that the catheter became dislodged from her vein and then was spilling the drugs into the surrounding tissue. That happens. That’s actually not uncommon, because the people who are pushing those drugs, who are actually pushing the syringes, you think they’re doctors? They’re not. They are nonmedical prison guards, and they are doing it from a separate room. They’re doing it from this little antechamber through 7 feet of tubing. And so when they’ve been asked, “How do you know how fast to push the plunger?” They’re like, “Well, we do it by feel.” There is no feel, not through 7 feet of tubing. So that’s one possibility, and it’s very consistent with what she said. She had complained of pain in the area of the injection, and she said, “It feels like my arm is about to burst.” That is exactly what I would expect if the drugs were infiltrating and going into her tissue, particularly pentobarbital, which is known to burn. Then there’s other possibilities that also go to our lack of knowledge. It could have been substandard drugs. This is all under the table. There’s no prescriptions. Compounding pharmacies are pharmacies, you can’t get a drug without a prescription, certainly not a sterile injectable drug. So what kinds of pharmacies are willing to completely disregard the pharmaceutical professional organizations which have all said to stay away from this? Who’s willing to do that for an under-the-table cash buy for drugs? And we know that those drugs have a 30 percent failure rate. We also know that Tennessee has a history of storing its drugs improperly. Compounded drugs have to be stored at negative 10 to negative 25 degrees celsius. According to a 2022 inspection report, when the drugstore was asked, “How are you storing these?” because they’re not in the logs, he said, “It’s supposed to be a little bit freezing.” That’s what they said. It all goes back to secrecy. We don’t know what went wrong because we don’t know where the drugs came from. We don’t know how they were stored. We don’t know who was pushing the plungers. We don’t know her medical condition. All of this seems like a problem, particularly when the state is at its most powerful moment. And to be clear, her attorneys warned this could happen. It’s not as though this couldn’t be anticipated, given what was known about her medical condition. Still, one might think that when the state botches an execution to this degree, there’s no talk of re-executing them. But tragically, the Supreme Court has weighed in on this issue, and the doctrine is really clear. So even if Pike wakes up from the critical condition in which she finds herself as a result of the state—as you say, in secret, without appropriate medical supervision—trying to kill her, they can just go back and try to kill her again. That is what the doctrine provides. Yes, states can try again. I suppose the only thing that would keep Tennessee from trying again is that there is a competency requirement for execution. It’s minimal, but you have to understand that the state is trying to kill you and what they’re trying to kill you for. So if she has severe brain damage, if she’s brain-dead, then that would raise competency questions. But just as far as, “Oh, we completely tortured and brutalized her before, let’s do it again,” the Supreme Court has blessed that. Our pro-life court. I think most Americans understand what the Eighth Amendment means, and that understanding has evolved over time. But you’re saying that the jurisprudence itself, the technologies, the sanitization of execution, every part of how we practice capital punishment has gone in the opposite direction. In other words, those things have been working very hard to make it OK to not think about this in terms of cruel and unusual punishment. Help me understand how the two pull in opposite directions. I think in some ways, this botched execution is emblematic of how everybody understands now what happens in that room, but the jurisprudence and the technologies have occluded that for most of us. When I first started the book project, I thought the question was: “Why are we, why are states so bad at killing?” Forget the clinical nature, but we know how to put down our pets. We know how physician-assisted suicide works. So why am I seeing all of these botched executions? But as I began to understand that, I asked a different question. I asked the question that you’re asking, which is: “Why are states sticking with lethal injection? What does lethal injection do for us?” Arizona spent $1.5 million on drugs for three executions and botched all three. They can’t get the medical professionals that they need to do it competently. They can’t get the drugs that they need. They can’t get access to the veins. The death row population is a geriatric population, with histories of poor health, and a lot of them are former IV-drug users. The bottom line is this demographic has notoriously weak veins. That is inherent. That is baked into lethal injection. They can’t get into the veins. Why are they sticking with this? My conclusion is lethal injection just does one thing well, only one. It hides the violence of state killing. With lethal injection, you don’t have the blood of the firing squad. You don’t have the sickly smell of burning flesh like you do with the electric chair, or people gasping with the gas mask, or with the gas chamber. With lethal injection, we get to tell ourselves that these people are just falling into a forever sleep. I was thinking of this saying that I had read, “The heart stops reluctantly.” And I was like, Oh, right, the heart stops reluctantly. Before a person is ready to die on their own, it takes physical force to stop the heart from beating. It takes violence to stop the heart from beating. And it could be a car accident or a homicide or an execution, but it takes violence. And what’s special about lethal injection is it hides the violence. It internalizes the violence. We can find it on autopsies, but the public isn’t paying attention to that. It really is out of sight, out of mind. That is the work that lethal injection does. I think it must be so important, so very important, because states have gone all out to try to maintain an execution method that looks like something else. Then we have Christa Pike, and it’s like, Oh, wait, this is a violent act. This is an atrocious act. Wait, we’re doing this? Who is she? What is her story? What do we get out of this? It raises all of the questions. And so my own hope is if there’s something good that comes of this, it will be spurring conversations like this one, of not only how we kill, but whether we kill. Meet the GOP Boogeymen
2026-10-06

Meet the GOP Boogeymen

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-06

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Hasan Piker, a Twitch streamer with a niche yet growing following, is about as left as you can get. The GOP has done everything it can to elevate some of his most objectionable takes— about communism, about Israel. But he’s also really electrified some younger voters. One way was by showing up at the Republican midterm convention on a giant screen in his underwear. Piker’s brother in liberalism is podcaster and YouTuber Adam Mockler. Like Piker, he’s been spending a lot of time talking to younger voters, getting them to debate him. He then attempts to translate what “the youths” are saying to the (presumably older) cable news viewer on networks like CNN. Recently, the New York Times had the headline “Calls for Generational Change Don’t Resonate in Voting Booth,” which doesn’t square with what Piker and Mockler are hearing. “I really disagree with that,” Mockler said. “Because we are seeing, right now, a massive shift in the power center of the Democratic Party towards younger voters, especially in the primaries.” Nearly 20 percent of Congress is leaving their seats. Longtime folks like Sens. Gary Peters, Dick Durbin, and more are packing it in. Does this mean a new generation is going to take their place? “The generational shift isn’t just about whether you’re an incumbent or a challenger,” Mockler said. “It can be communication on social media. It can be the way that you promise to use the power, once you actually have it, to change the lives of Americans. Or it can just be authenticity.” On a recent episode of What Next, host Mary Harris spoke to Piker and Mockler about whether the November midterm could be one of generational change, and whether that change is actually here already. This conversation has been edited and condensed for clarity. Mary Harris: I’m just going to take this back a little bit. I feel like ever since Joe Biden took to a debate stage and face-planted, Democrats in particular have been having this “generational change” conversation. And even though Kamala Harris eventually replaced Biden on the ballot, I feel this question—what if he passed the torch sooner?—is looming. And we’re about to have this midterm election, and it basically started off with the generational question. You had David Hogg, the activist survivor of the Marjory Stoneman Douglas school shooting, basically saying, “I want to primary people. We need a generational change.” It was interesting to me because even his message was a little bit muddied. In the beginning, he was explaining why he was supporting an 80-year-old incumbent in Illinois who eventually stepped out of her race. It shows to me how this generational change thing, you can’t define it by data points so well. But I do feel like what happened with Hogg was this moment where I realized how rocky things were going to be for the Dems. Are things better now? Adam Mockler: Forcibly so, yes. The voter base has changed the incentives that politicians follow, and the incentive is no longer to take money from special interest groups and do scripted, consultant-written television hits every single Sunday. Now it’s these more nimble, flexible videos, where you’re talking about the corruption of the system. I think the voters shifted the incentives of what politicians have to do in a campaign. How do you see that? Are there particular races where you see this happening? Hasan Piker: Yeah, absolutely. Melat Kiros in Denver unseated a long-term incumbent. Abdul El-Sayed is obviously the most nationally significant race. But Darializa Avila Chevalier unseated another incumbent, the head of the congressional Hispanic caucus, Adriano Espaillat. That’s a deep-blue district. But then Michigan is, quote-unquote, a purple state, or a swing state. I oppose that framing. Piker: Michigan is never supposed to be a swing state. We turned it into a swing state with our ineptitude, with our incompetence, with NAFTA, and then also by not leaning into the populist rage that people were feeling. And that created a unique opportunity for the Republicans to basically farm that outrage and redirect it back to marginalized groups and generate a more convincing message that they were going to be the anti-war side—twice. Once against Hillary Clinton and also once against Kamala Harris, when it’s clear that they were never going to be anti-war. I mean, they are warmongers. They always start way more wars than even the Democrats do. This was very frustrating to observe from afar. That’s why I think Michigan is a blue state. It’s only turned into a swing state because we’ve missed. I look at Abdul El-Sayed, and I actually see both the case for and against generational change. The New York Times just did a survey, and they showed that he has more support among young people than they’ve ever seen before—like 80-plus percent support from 18- to 29-year-olds, which is amazing, right? But that rests on them turning out. And then there was [Hasan Piker’s] involvement in the race, which has become a huge lightning rod because El-Sayed needs Jewish voters to turn out, and a lot of more conservative Jewish voters have looked at your involvement in that race and they’ve basically said, “You need to distance yourself from Hasan Piker.” Do you have any regrets, looking back at when were you out there? Piker: No, I do not have any. I did not have any concerns with my involvement whatsoever. I don’t think that it was the wrong thing to do. I think that we showed once again that we were able to defeat $70 million in outside expenditures. But barely, right? The polls said that Hasan would perform way ahead, and he was much closer. I think that as we get closer, that’s where people are like, Oh, he’s making us nervous. Piker: Look, money in politics is obviously very important, right? It’s also very successful. I believe the stat is like 96 percent of the time, the side that has more money wins. And that makes sense. That means that you can set the narrative, you can set the agenda, you can disparage your opponent. Then your opponent has to spend a lot of money defending himself. Money plays a pretty formative role in these races. Adam, I want to get you in here because I feel like another thing we’ve sort of danced around is what’s happening in Texas, where we have James Talarico. Here’s what I see when I look at that: I wonder if that race is about generational change or about a terrible Republican candidate on the other side. James Talarico is young, and he is pulling ahead of Ken Paxton, which is kind of amazing because Beto O’Rourke wasn’t doing that. But Paxton is a remarkably bad candidate. Even Donald Trump doesn’t seem to like him. Talk to me about this race. Mockler: It can be both/and. Obviously Talarico is a really strong candidate while Paxton is a very unique candidate. But it goes back to the framework I laid out at the beginning. You have to look at how Talarico is promising to use power once he gets it and how he’s communicating online. There’s this really viral video of Talarico calling Paxton’s cellphone number and asking him to debate him on Fox News, and Paxton doesn’t answer. He’s running from the debates. He’s openly admitting that he basically gave a plea deal to a [sex offender] named Adam Hoffman. He was impeached by his own party. All of this is true. But on the other side of that, Talarico has very impressively used digital media, and look at his campaign promises. They are revolving around making the system work for the average voter. Many young people right now are skeptical that the system can even respond to us in a meaningful way, even if we did vote someone in. So, Talarico is proving that he will use this power in a very responsible way and to help people. A few weeks ago, Talarico went on Jimmy Kimmel’s show—that has 15 million views on YouTube. That’s using the new media, or at least the decentralized YouTube platform, to push your narrative. He makes short-form clips. He is a fighter who is bringing the fight to Ken Paxton, demanding that there’s some sort of debate. And overall, I think just taking money out of politics, advocating for a ban on gerrymandering, Supreme Court reform. The GOP seems legitimately scared of Talarico. A lot of the candidates really don’t want to debate. Piker: Yeah. Mike Rogers ran away too. Mike Rogers ran away from the first debate. And he pointed to the fact that Roop Raj, who’s a very well-respected, credentialed reporter in Michigan, was biased in Abdul’s direction. I think it was a little bit racist for him to imply that. Because Roop Raj is brown, and they are obviously presenting Abdul as this, like, scary brown guy. Having said that, I think voters are seeing that. They’re not responding positively to that. And the cult, the base of power, that the Republicans have is shrinking every single day. I do think we have to look at a couple of examples that argue against this idea that whatever happens in November is about generational change. I would start with Ohio, where we have Sherrod Brown, who’s in a good position to get back into the Senate. And he was there for years. And then you can look at North Carolina, where Thom Tillis is out, and Democrats nominated former Gov. Roy Cooper. That race is going so well for him that the GOP recently pulled money out of it. Those are two races where it is kind of about the same old Democratic Party. What do you say to that? Mockler: I think that Roy Cooper is a really strong candidate. If we back up, you can’t generalize with every single race that the nonincumbent is going to win. Different politicians have different coalitions. The coalition that Andy Beshear has in Kentucky is far different than the coalition that Zohran Mamdani built when he was winning in New York. Of course Ohio is going to look slightly different than Michigan, where Abdul El-Sayed is likely to win. There are going to be exceptions to the rule, but oftentimes you see even these candidates leveraging social media and talking policy in a different way. Piker: Look, Sherrod Brown is probably one of the more progressive Democrats, and he was a resilient force in Ohio politics for decades. And part of that was because of his uncompromising values. When it came to labor, he’s always been the guy backed by the state labor unions. And that is now giving him another unique opportunity to take a shot and to get back into office at a time when people are looking for that. “Generational change” is people looking for real fighters. It is people that are demanding change in general. And yeah, if it works, it works. I’m not going to oppose more establishment-aligned Democrats winning their seats in places like North Carolina. I don’t want Republicans to be in office. I don’t want them to be in power. And I think that’s the beauty of democracy and is the beauty of this big-tent coalition that we’re trying to build here. There’s gonna be people like Zohran Mamdani, and then sometimes there’s going to be people like Roy Cooper as well. Mockler: Or Liz Cheney, unfortunately. I mean, Hasan, every podcast or debate I go into over the past few months, people try to grill me on you, and they’re like, “Do you disavow Hasan Piker?” And it’s obviously not that simple. We disagree on certain things, but— Piker: I disavow myself all the time. Every day I wake up, I’m like, “I disavow myself.” Mockler: Does Hasan have comments in 2019 that I disagree with? Yeah, sure. Even he disagrees with them. Does Hasan have policy positions that we can talk about in a rational way? Sure. But I keep getting asked about this. And I think the reality is: When we’re up against fascism, you have to look back at World War II. In World War II, they had such a broad coalition that you had, like, the communists, the socialists, you had the liberals, and you had even more Republican-leaning people all coalesced together to beat the fascists. So, if it’s Hasan Piker, if it’s Liz Cheney, whoever wants to stand up against this current administration, then they are welcome in the tent. Piker: We have to build allegiances. We don’t have the opportunity to just cast aside potential allies because of some twisted moral-purity attitude that we have. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. Anti-Abortion Activists Finally Overreached at the Supreme Court
2026-10-06

Anti-Abortion Activists Finally Overreached at the Supreme Court

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-10-06

Visit Original Website
Sign up for Executive Dysfunction, a weekly newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Abortion is back at the Supreme Court. Louisiana, Arkansas, and Alabama filed a new challenge last week to shield laws, statutes that protect people in states where abortion is legal from out-of-state civil and criminal consequences. Shield laws are a major reason that conservative states have struggled to enforce abortion bans. The Food and Drug Administration currently allows patients to access telehealth abortion. Shield laws have given a network of providers the confidence to provide telemedicine services to patients even in states where abortion is illegal. Louisiana and the other plaintiffs argue that the entire scheme is illegal and unconstitutional. The Supreme Court has original jurisdiction in fights between the states like this one, but there is reason to doubt whether this will be the suit to bring down shield laws. Some of the concerns it raises, especially about clashes between state laws and the full faith and credit clause, are inevitably headed for a reckoning at the Supreme Court. The full faith and credit clause has long been understood to require states to honor public acts, records, and judicial decisions of their sister states. Abortion opponents have long argued that shield laws fly in the face of this principle. The timing of this case still seems premature. There are ongoing attempts to sue shield doctors or extradite them to ban states. For example, Louisiana Attorney General Liz Murrill sought to extradite two shield doctors, Remy Coeytaux of California and Margaret Carpenter of New York. A number of attorneys general have also filed suit against individual shield providers. Texas’ own civil suit against Carpenter for violating the state’s abortion laws is the furthest along. When Carpenter refused to travel to the state, a Texas court entered a default judgment against her including more than $100,000 in civil penalties, but a New York clerk refused to enforce that judgment against Carpenter, citing the state’s shield law. A state trial judge in New York dismissed Texas’ suit on a similar basis. An appeal in the case is still pending. No shield-state Supreme Court has weighed in on how to resolve conflicts between shield laws and abortion bans. That means that the Supreme Court may want to let the issue percolate more in the lower courts before stepping in—and to see how irrevocable the conflict between the states truly is. The red states’ full-faith-and-credit argument is hardly a slam dunk. The Supreme Court has carved out an exception in cases involving the full faith and credit clause establishing that states don’t have to enforce out-of-state judgments that penalize offenses against the state or members of its public. That’s exactly what Louisiana and its fellow states seem to be doing here. Some of the other claims in the suit are even more of a reach. Take the states’ claims about public nuisance. The law defines a public nuisance as unlawful conduct that affects the rights of the public. That would classically include activities like burning toxic trash or blocking a highway. Beyond that, the law of nuisance has hardly been a strategic coup for those seeking to use it. Take the example of opioids. State, local, and tribal governments turned to the idea of public nuisance to seek accountability for the harms of the opioid epidemic, arguing that the marketing and distribution of opioids had devastating public-health impacts. Even though everyone agrees that the opioid epidemic has been a scourge, the result in these cases has been mixed. The litigation against pharmaceutical companies led to settlement agreements totaling some $50 billion, but the theory met an uneven reception in the courts, with some judges rejecting it. Deeming shield laws or abortion pills to be a public nuisance seems to be completely implausible. Most Americans think abortion should be legal and support the idea that the right to choose abortion is protected by the Constitution. Shield laws were passed by democratically elected legislators. It is hard to see the Supreme Court deeming either one a nuisance. The politics of the suit are also puzzling. The Trump administration has worked tirelessly to push off confrontations about abortion medication until after the midterm, recognizing that Republicans don’t tend to benefit when abortion is in the spotlight. Congress and the White House have largely succeeded in that task, even as several major federal cases and an FDA review of mifepristone loom large. It is bizarre to see Republican states intent on pushing the issue to the forefront now with this case, even if it risks hurting Republicans already in serious jeopardy of losing control of Congress. The conservative Supreme Court supermajority will certainly be open to constitutional challenges to shield laws, even if the ultimate outcome of those challenges remains in doubt. But this case won’t likely go the way Republican AGs are planning. It’s noteworthy that not every prominent attorney general joined this filing; some of the most visible, like the attorneys general of Texas and Florida, are strikingly absent. There seems to be a reason for that: The threat to shield laws is real, but this case may not be the best vehicle to take them down. The result may be a continued sense of complacency among Americans who think that the status quo on abortion cannot change because it has remained the same since shield laws first passed in 2022. That is a mistake. This suit may not be the one that transforms abortion in America, but the odds are that after the midterm, something else will. When one datacenter is no longer enough
2026-10-07

When one datacenter is no longer enough

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
SPONSORED POST: Why training AI across multiple sites is creating a new networking challenge Training the largest AI models is no longer simply a question of adding more GPUs. As clusters grow, power availability is becoming a hard physical constraint, forcing infrastructure teams to consider how a single training workload can operate across multiple data centres. That creates a very different networking problem. Traditional datacenter interconnect was designed to move traffic between sites. AI training demands something more exacting: huge, synchronous flows, minimal packet loss and tightly coordinated communication between GPUs. If one part of the cluster stalls, the impact can ripple across the entire job. In this interview, The Register’s Tim Phillips speaks with Rakesh Chopra, SVP, Silicon and Systems Architecture, Cisco Fellow, about what Cisco calls the “Scale-Across” imperative and why it believes distributed AI infrastructure requires a new approach to routing. Rakesh explains how the demands of AI are changing the role of the network, from simple transport to an integral part of the compute system. He discusses the challenge of making geographically separate facilities behave more like a single deterministic machine, and why buffering, high-speed coherent optics and tightly integrated silicon matter when workloads span long-distance fiber links. The conversation also looks inside the cluster itself. Rakesh outlines how Cisco’s Silicon One architecture and Intelligent Collective Networking are designed to manage synchronized bursts of GPU traffic, reduce bottlenecks and improve job completion time. Power efficiency is another central theme, including the trade-off between network consumption and the energy available to GPUs. Security and longevity also come into focus. The interview covers hardware-accelerated MACsec and IPsec, as well as the role of programmability in helping infrastructure adapt as AI workloads continue to evolve. For infrastructure and datacenter leaders planning for larger, more distributed AI environments, the discussion offers a practical view of the networking challenges that emerge once one site is no longer enough. Hang on. How about not chucking tens of thousands more satellites into space? www.theregister.com - Articles
Europe's mobile operators sell fewer phones but gain share as budget market shrivels
2026-10-07

Europe's mobile operators sell fewer phones but gain share as budget market shrivels

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Contract purchases hold up better than independent retail sales, with Apple overtaking Samsung in the carrier channel European mobile operators are gaining a slightly larger share of smartphone sales, not because they are selling more devices, but because other sales channels are shrinking faster. This is according to market watcher Counterpoint Research, which says that sales of new smartphones through European operators were down by 4 percent during the second quarter of this year, compared with the same period a year ago. But device sales through the open market fared much worse, declining by 11 percent. This refers to phones that are bought outright from independent retailers or directly from manufacturers, which are typically SIM-free and unlocked so the customer can choose which network they hook up to. Operators accounted for 36 percent of European smartphone sales in the second quarter, up from 35 percent a year earlier. The open market still represented the majority. As The Register has covered numerous times this year, mobile phones have been hit by the AI-driven memory crisis, which has led to a shortage of the DRAM and NAND flash components needed for consumer kit like smartphones and PCs. The resulting rise in prices has been felt most keenly at the lower end of the market, effectively making it commercially unviable to produce budget devices, according to some reports. Phones sold through operators tend to be higher-end models than those bought on the open market. Contracts spread the handset cost over monthly payments, making expensive devices more accessible. Apple played a key part in this slight shift, Counterpoint says, overtaking Samsung as the brand with the greatest market share in Europe's operator channel. Motorola and vivo are highlighted as the fastest-growing bands in the same segment. Counterpoint associate director Jan Stryjak said operators were likely to keep gaining market share for the next few quarters at least, as the lower end of the market continues to be impacted by the memory crisis. "However, we are seeing a steady increase in promotional activity by independent retailers compared to operators, which could mitigate some of the price rises and provide additional stimulus to the open market," he said. Or perhaps more buyers might turn to the second-hand market instead, which has been predicted to see a rise in trade of 12 percent this year, as rising prices make new phones less affordable. Counterpoint forecast that 80 percent of top-flight smartphones will feature agentic AI capabilities by 2027, meaning they will have software agents making decisions and carrying out actions on the owner's behalf. ® Hang on. Nine in 10 VMware customers eye the exit as licensing bills bite
2026-10-07

Nine in 10 VMware customers eye the exit as licensing bills bite

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Rimini Street-sponsored survey finds 48% have no plans to move workloads to Broadcom's preferred subscription platform Nine out of ten respondents to a survey of VMware customers say higher licensing costs were prompting them to consider alternatives. Unisphere Research surveyed 269 executives, managers, and professionals at VMware user organizations between December 2025 and February 2026. The study, commissioned by third-party support provider Rimini Street, also found that 54 percent cited the end of perpetual license support as a reason to evaluate other options. The study showed cost remains the leading driver of virtualization roadmap decisions. Seventy-three percent said savings were a top priority. Respondents also identified barriers to change: operational complexity (40 percent), multi-vendor management challenges (38 percent), securing the increased attack surface (37 percent), and team skills requirements (37 percent). Users were looking for choice, flexibility, and control over their virtualization strategies, said Keith Costello, chief operating officer with Rimini Street, which sells third-party support for VMware. "These survey findings reinforce the need for practical, vendor-independent strategies that put business priorities back on the client's terms." Sixty percent of respondents were considering a multi-hypervisor strategy. Forty-eight percent said they did not currently plan to move any assets to VMware Cloud Foundation (VCF), Broadcom's subscription platform and preferred migration path. Broadcom agreed to buy VMware in 2022. By November 2024, Gartner was arguing that customers were worse off because of the acquisition. In May 2025, cloud customer group the European Cloud Competition Observatory (ECCO) and cloud trade association CISPE claimed that Broadcom had upped VMware licensing costs to between eight and 15 times their previous levels since it acquired VMware. At the time, Broadcom said it was working to advance the European Union's sovereign cloud objectives and enable enterprises to "accelerate innovation, provide more choice, and address their most complex technology challenges." In June, UK retail giant Tesco said it was replacing VMware with an alternative product and pressing ahead with its licensing lawsuit against the virtualization pioneer's parent company, Broadcom, with a High Court trial scheduled no earlier than November 2027. Last month, Gartner predicted that more than half of enterprises would begin proofs of concept for alternatives to their VMware deployments by 2029, while still rating the Broadcom business unit a leader in two major markets. "By 2029, 55 percent of enterprises will initiate proofs of concept for alternative distributed hybrid infrastructure products to replace their VMware-based deployments and embrace hybrid cloud infrastructure delivery, up from 25 percent in 2026," it said. ® Hang on. Dutch tax office ditches Microsoft 365 cloud for on-premises alternative
2026-10-07

Dutch tax office ditches Microsoft 365 cloud for on-premises alternative

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Email and calendars move in-house in 2027, with European open source tools to follow The Netherlands Tax and Customs Administration has abandoned its planned Microsoft 365 cloud migration in favor of services hosted on infrastructure it controls, alongside European open source alternatives. Email and calendars will move on-premises in 2027, followed by storage and collaboration services later that year and in 2028. The letter to parliament from Eelco Eerenberg, State Secretary for Finance, follows an earlier decision to pause the Microsoft 365 rollout. The tax authority selected Microsoft 365 in 2025, but the decision attracted criticism. In June, the Dutch Advisory Council on ICT Assessment recommended rolling back the cloud services rollout, which had reached about 5,000 of the 47,500 employees across the tax, customs, and benefits services, rather than completing it as planned by the end of 2026. According to the board, the design of Microsoft 365 in the public cloud "falls short on information security, information processing and future-proofing." On security, the board noted concerns about sending confidential documents, which currently travel over government networks, via American servers. The board also criticized reliance on standard Microsoft encryption for sensitive data rather than Double Key Encryption, which would give the administration greater control. End-to-end encryption was not enabled by default for one-to-one Teams calls and was unavailable for group calls under the chosen configuration. To be fair to Microsoft, many of these concerns (for example, around encryption) stem from the design selected. However, others are harder to dismiss. On future-proofing, other concerns included vendor lock-in and the risk of reduced flexibility and sovereignty from choosing a single-supplier solution with one American (cloud) provider. There was also a concern about a lack of an exit strategy for when the Tax and Customs Administration wanted to move on to more sovereign alternatives. In this week's letter, Eerenberg wrote: "In 2027, the provision for mail and calendar will be realized on-premises. Later in 2027 and 2028, European open source solutions for personal storage and collaboration functionalities will follow. This allows us to work toward a sustainable and more autonomous solution for these functionalities." Increased datacenter capacity has made the on-premises option possible, Eerenberg said. The revised approach will reuse existing licenses where possible to limit additional investment. The reversal creates complications for records management, however. Some planned capabilities in the Microsoft 365 package are unavailable on-premises, and the administration is still assessing how to replace them. Microsoft is no stranger to public bodies in the Netherlands, and a recent report showed that a majority used the company's cloud services. However, concerns about US authorities' ability to compel disclosure of data held by US providers under the CLOUD Act have loomed large in recent years. The risk has prompted European governments to consider reducing their dependence on US providers and moving to more sovereign solutions. It is early days, and agencies report teething trouble switching. A colorful report from Politico quoted an unnamed European Commission staffer describing the Teams alternative supplied by Element as "absolute shit" on an internal message board. The report does not establish how widespread those complaints are. The Matrix protocol underpinning Element's software also powers the French public sector messaging service, Tchap. Element boss Matthew Hodgson was upbeat about the European Commission deployment, telling The Register: "We've only had positive and constructive feedback from our actual customers; the leaders and IT functions that are driving sovereignty. "There's inevitably a few unhappy change-resistant users in large deployments, but we work in lockstep with our customers to gather feedback and incorporate it, especially during the trial phase." As for sovereignty more widely in Europe, Hodgson said: "European governments are moving to alternatives that give them greater sovereignty. They can't leave themselves exposed to being deplatformed, and they recognise that traditional centralised technology isn't resilient enough for an increasingly unpredictable geopolitical climate." Frank Karlitschek, founder and CEO of Nextcloud, a vendor of storage and collaboration software, said: "It is great to see Dutch tax authorities have decided to work toward a sovereign digital workplace. This will benefit the resilience of their operations. "European Public sector organizations mostly use software from a few large, proprietary providers, which has led to strong dependencies. "Organizations have recognized the risks of digital dependencies and are looking for better options to have full control over their data, infrastructure, and underlying technology. The organizations now aim to build a more resilient IT stack." Karlitschek noted a trend forming across Europe. According to Nextcloud, it added more than two million sovereign workspace seats in 2025. Hang on. How about not chucking tens of thousands more satellites into space? FortiBleed still a bleeding nuisance as FBI confirms ongoing attacks
2026-10-07

FortiBleed still a bleeding nuisance as FBI confirms ongoing attacks

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Tens of thousands more victims and more ransomware groups getting in on the act The FBI and US Secret Service (USSS) say criminals using credentials linked to the FortiBleed campaign are locking organizations out of their Fortinet firewalls. The agencies published a joint advisory on Tuesday, citing SOCRadar's verification of more than 86,644 compromised devices across 194 countries. "Based on initial responses, some victims may get locked out of their Fortinet devices if the threat actor either deletes or changes the password for original accounts on the system," their advisory [PDF] states. "During the initial intrusion, threat actors create new accounts not previously on the device. In certain cases, threat actors delete existing accounts to block organizations from accessing affected devices and to maintain persistence on the system while attempting lateral movement within the environment." The campaign targets internet-facing FortiGate firewalls and SSL VPN gateways. Criminals use credentials from earlier breaches and infostealer logs for credential stuffing and password spraying, then extract password hashes from compromised devices and crack them offline using GPU-accelerated clusters. The agencies urged organizations to restrict internet-facing management access, terminate active administrative and VPN sessions, reset passwords, and enable phishing-resistant multi-factor authentication. The advisory also links FortiBleed to ransomware campaigns, saying initial access brokers supplied compromised-network access to ransomware affiliates. The Register previously reported on the connection, identified by SOCRadar. The current evidence points to affiliates working for the INC/Lynx and Payload ransomware groups making use of the credentials, and SOCRadar said in July that it had seen at least 12 confirmed ransomware attacks stemming from FortiBleed. The agencies encouraged victims to report incidents, while noting that organizations were not obliged to provide information in response to this advisory. The FBI and the Secret Service said victim reports could help identify indicators of compromise and warned against paying ransoms. ® Hang on. Rails originator roasted over Rust boosterism
2026-10-07

Rails originator roasted over Rust boosterism

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
David Heinemeier Hansson's agent-coded bakeoff produced curious results Last month at Rails World 2026, Ruby on Rails creator David Heinemeier Hansson gave a relentlessly optimistic keynote in which he suggested AI has all but eliminated the need for developers to manually write code. But perhaps DHH, as he is known online, shouldn’t delete his code editor quite yet. On Sunday, DHH posted about a test in which he had frontier agents implement and optimize his company’s Campfire group chat app in three languages: Elixir, Go, and Rust. He then compared the runtime performance of those versions with the original Ruby on Rails implementation. DHH’s post includes data that seemingly demonstrates the efficiency of the Rust programming language, and AI agent coding itself. But many observers came to another conclusion: Don't trust DHH’s benchmarks. As of press time, the post and accompanying chart garnered over 900,000 views. It showed the Rust rewrite of the chat app outperformed others by a wide margin on five tasks, including searching or posting a message. DHH did not say which AI services he used for these rewrites, but noted that each rewrite took only a few hours, as the original code itself, largely written in Ruby, is relatively compact. Many observers, notably advocates of other languages, quickly surmised from the source material that DHH had optimized the Rust results but didn't do the same for the other languages he tested. Rewriting applications in Rust, often with the help of AI agents, seems to be a popular undertaking these days, with Microsoft's Copilot runtime, the pnpm JavaScript installer, and the Bun JavaScript toolchain all getting the Rust treatment. In DHH’s test, the Rust implementation handily bested the others across all five workloads, sometimes by a wide margin. To serve a “room page,” for instance, Rust knocked out 36,260 requests per second. In contrast, Ruby on Rails was only able to fulfill a paltry 241 requests, while Elixir delivered 722 requests, and even runner-up Golang only knocked out 3,860 requests a second. Rust executed the other tasks with similar alacrity, easily besting the other languages DHH used. The takeaway was obvious to DHH: Rewriting an application in Rust, or better yet having AI agents do the job, may very well result in major performance improvements for an app. DHH suggested that many factors go into choosing a programming language for a particular job, and execution speed is not the sole criterion. Rust can be agonizingly slow to compile, and for many, writing or even reading Rust code is an arduous job. DHH himself began developing Rails in 2003 while building Basecamp in the then relatively obscure Ruby programming language to ease the developer experience. He doesn’t seem to mind that Ruby ranked the slowest in his own tests. “I didn't care about [performance] when the pay-off was huge in terms of productivity and developer joy.” But AI agents don’t give a hoot about joy. “You really have to bury your head in the sand not to see that when agents write the code and validate the output, we need to adjust to a new reality,” he wrote. DHH’s ad-hoc test was quickly met with skepticism, criticism and outright mockery, especially on X. (It’s as if DHH’s conservative views on immigration and his work to bring yet another Linux desktop into the world didn’t give him enough headaches). “What worries me about the future of programming is you see this and don’t immediately know there must be something wrong with the results or what you actually measured,” wrote prominent Google engineer Jaana Dogan in an X response to DHH’s tests. Dogan did not elaborate on the test’s shortcomings, but many others, especially advocates of other languages, were more than happy to provide details. One Elixir advocate called DHH’s implementation “moronic,” while a couple of others actually rewrote the Elixir code to make its performance comparable to Rust’s. After that rewrite, the Elixir version of the app bested Rust’s performance on some tasks, despite further third-party Rust optimizations. DHH expressed surprise that others would assume a high-level language like Elixir or Ruby could even compete with a language like Rust, which runs closer to the metal. But he also noted on X that “It's not exactly extra points for Elixir if frontier agents can't find the magic ‘go fast’ switches.” Elsewhere, observers noted that the Rust rewrite got a lot more post-coding attention than the other implementations of Go and Elixir, both of which seemed to be quickly wrapped up after the initial agent requests. “You're not benchmarking languages, you're benchmarking how much attention each port got,” one observer wrote on X. Golang advocates were likewise unamused. “The benchmark was unfair. More time was put into the Rust code while the rest were one-shotted,” one wrote on X. Someone else even made their own chart showing how much more the Rust code was tuned compared to the quick passes on the other languages. None of these rebuttals actually negates DHH’s advocacy of using agents to code. But clearly, skipping a manual review of the resulting work will still leave a lot of potential gains on the table. ® Hang on. How about not chucking tens of thousands more satellites into space? The Verge
The scariest thing about gray-market peptides is how little we know
2026-10-07

The scariest thing about gray-market peptides is how little we know

The Verge | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
Researchers found that BPC-157 users tend to wait two months before disclosing usage to doctors — if they disclose at all. Researchers found that BPC-157 users tend to wait two months before disclosing usage to doctors — if they disclose at all. This is Optimizer, a weekly newsletter sent from Verge senior reviewer Victoria Song that dissects and discusses the latest gizmos and potions that swear they’re going to change your life. Opt in for Optimizer here. Finding concrete data about gray-market peptides is a Sisyphean task. Unlike bona fide clinical drug trials, there are no official numbers. There’s little to no safety data. Some peptides have more anecdotal evidence than others, but a stranger on r/peptides is, perhaps, not an ideal information source. The fact is most people partaking in a dubiously legal activity aren’t going to shout it from the rooftops. What we do know about many of these unregulated, unapproved substances is simply this: A lot of people are taking them, unsupervised by medical professionals. Take BPC-157 — one half of the infamous “Wolverine” stack. The BPC in BPC-157 stands for “body protection compound.” The substance was originally found in gastric juice in 1993 and is thought to promote injury recovery. It supposedly works by creating new blood vessels, promoting cell growth, and repairing damaged tissues like tendons, muscles, and ligaments. You know, Wolverine-y stuff. Sounds great. Except it’s not approved by any regulatory body, has primarily been studied in animals, and has limited clinical evidence in humans. One systematic review from 2025 found only 35 preclinical studies and a single clinical study for BPC-157 in humans. Nevertheless, BPC-157 is on a lot of people’s minds. That same systematic review states that, in 2024, there were over 50 million views for BPC-157 videos on YouTube and TikTok. According to Google Trends, there’s been a 1,600 percent increase in traditional web searches for BPC-157 in the United States in the last five years. That jumps to over 5,000 percent for YouTube searches. As for real-life usage data — it’s impossible to find reliable numbers. That said, a recent preprint study attempted to capture how real-world BPC-157 use has increased. The study, yet to go through peer review, is a retrospective look at anonymized electronic health records using a large language model to detect mentions of the peptide in clinical notes. This itself is an imperfect method, as BPC-157 has no insurance code or a uniform way for medical providers to indicate a patient is using it. Even so, the preprint highlights interesting — and concerning — trends. “[BPC-157 usage] was kind of benign during the pandemic and the deep end of the pandemic, but it’s skyrocketed over the last few quarters. Over the last three years, the use has increased significantly,” says Venky Soundararajan, one of the study’s authors and chief scientific officer of nference, an AI startup that uses LLMs to find insights from clinical records. Put plainly, the study found roughly 54 confirmed patients using BPC-157 in Q1 2022. (The real number is likely much higher, but this is a snapshot based on the records the researchers had access to.) That number jumped to 1,018 in Q1 2026. According to Soundararajan, about 33 percent of patients who used BPC-157 didn’t pursue any other therapies or drug use. Roughly half, however, stacked it with other substances. (Chief among those were testosterone, non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs, and TB-500, the other half of the Wolverine stack, which also supports tissue repair.) He also noted that the majority of patients were taking BPC-157 orally, followed by injections. “The average BPC-157 user who’s showing up to the doctor is getting younger and younger — they’re in the upper 40s as an average age and is very likely a Caucasian white patient,” says Soundararajan. “And from the time the product is [first] used and the time they show up to discuss their use with a doctor, at least two months has elapsed.” As for the type of doctors, BPC-157 is most commonly reported to family practice and internal medicine, followed by gastroenterologists and orthopedic surgeons. Even so, this data has flaws, acknowledges Soundararajan. For example, he pointed out that 96 percent of patients who disclosed their usage were Caucasian, compared to the 2 percent that were African American. That’s a dramatic gap from what the researchers would’ve expected statistically based on the source data, which was 78 percent Caucasian and 17 percent African American. This doesn’t mean African Americans aren’t taking BPC-157 — they may simply feel less confident talking about it with doctors compared to Caucasians. As for why someone might feel that way, I’ve conducted interviews with peptide users who say they don’t want judgment, don’t trust their doctor is knowledgeable about the subject, or fear it may lead to repercussions with insurance. Another problem, Soundararajan says, is that this preprint can’t really give reliable insights as to BPC-157’s efficacy in humans or safety. Only a small subset reported adverse reactions — roughly 2 to 3 percent. However, the problem is that data is anecdotal. In a clinical setting, he explains, researchers and doctors evaluate adverse reactions on a graded scale. Minor, manageable adverse effects might get reported in records, but severe cases requiring hospitalizations may not. Pain, he says, is also not an objective assessment. In the study, most of the positive effects of BPC-157 were self-reported by patients. However, physicians rarely intervened to say they also saw an improvement in the patient’s condition. Also, 66 percent of the cases showed no documented response on whether BPC-157 worked or not — only that the patient had tried it. “The medical record has a bias. Obviously, there are those who are using the product for performance enhancement who are not talking about it,” says Soundararajan. For example, the top recorded reason for BPC-157 use is pain management. On the one hand, that indicates BPC-157 users may be turning to the substance because there’s an unmet need that legal therapies can’t address. But, he says, those are self-selecting patients. It’s possible that only patients with positive experiences were likely to talk about their usage in an official setting. Pro-peptide users might say this is evidence that BPC-157 works. Some might even conclude from this preprint that, given their individual circumstances, the risk calculus is worth a go. But from a regulatory standpoint, this simply isn’t good enough. Perhaps the scariest thing is that even Soundararajan can’t say whether the preprint truly says anything about BPC-157. The patients in the preprint obtained the peptide from gray-market sources, so it’s possible that some were taking another substance entirely. There’s always the chance that the positive effects recorded are simply the placebo effect. We’ll never know. But this is the sort of information physicians, researchers, journalists, regulators, and the peptide-curious have to go off of. A fuzzy picture that tells part of a story, but not the entirety. Imperfect data like this is better than nothing, but it raises more questions than it half-answers. The only thing that anyone can truly conclude from a study like this? We don’t know enough, but BPC-157 isn’t going away anytime soon. Doctors will have to start thinking about how to talk to patients who do turn to the gray market. “The reason we are handicapped, not only us but others when they try to replicate these studies, is that they’ll be relying on clinical notes alone,” says Soundararajan. “You need to make it easy for the healthcare provider to structure the conversations that they’re having with patients for unapproved products. This is one thing where we think regulation should change. We believe health record manufacturers need to make it easy for doctors to record. Who’s the source? Where did you get the product from?” Even if that helps paint a clearer picture of gray-market use, that, too, is a stopgap solution. One thing that everyone can agree on is that both prospective studies and gold-standard clinical trials are necessary. It’s “just” a matter of who will pony up the millions required. And it’s not isolated to BPC-157. This goes for all the commonly used gray-market peptides. Until then, incomplete data is all we have. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. On Wednesday, Disney announced that viewers will be able to tune in to Super Bowl LXI on its streaming service when the game kicks off on February 14th, 2027. Disney Plus subscribers in the US will see the same Super Bowl broadcast airing on ESPN and ABC simultaneously, featuring coverage from Joe Buck, Troy Aikman, Lisa Salters, and Laura Rutledge. The streamer will also air the Super Bowl in more than 55 international markets, including Latin America, South Africa, and Australia. This year’s Super Bowl streamed live on NBC’s Peacock, contributing to a boost in subscribers. In the months leading up to Super Bowl LXI, Disney Plus subscribers will also be able to watch two Monday Night Football games. The first game takes place on October 26th between the Dallas Cowboys and the Philadelphia Eagles, but the second matchup has not yet been announced. A free mod for Titanfall 2 makes it one of the best VR games ever
2026-10-07

A free mod for Titanfall 2 makes it one of the best VR games ever

The Verge | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
I thought VR Titanfall would make me sick — instead, I’m having a blast. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. Titanfall has a special place in my heart. I’ve never felt such a rush in gaming since — leaping into the air with a jump pack, running on walls, summoning a giant mechanized battle suit that falls like a meteor from the sky, then letting one of its giant hands stuff me into the cockpit for a supersized skirmish. How could that possibly work in virtual reality? It sounds like a formula for pain, nausea, distress. But no: Titanfall 2 VR is one of the top five VR games I’ve ever played — despite being a free mod, released by a solo developer, who’s only spent a single month working on it so far. Now I’m running on walls, blasting bad guys from every angle, shouldering a scoped anti-materiel sniper rifle nearly as big as Cloud Strife’s infamous Buster Sword, all in virtual reality — and, yes, leaping into a hulking Titan to dish out damage while snatching enemy missiles out of the air. I can look around the cockpit and move the Titan’s giant arms realistically with my own two hands. I can look down and watch my feet do the wall-running. A few hours in, I only feel the familiar twinge of simulator sickness if I shove the analog stick too far and turn my Titan too fast. The entire experience immediately brought me back to Capcom’s Resident Evil 4 VR, one of the only other full-length classic AAA games that has ever been remade for virtual reality and a completely comfortable experience for me. Why have so few decade-old games been given this treatment? Why aren’t dedicated VR games this good? That’s what Jordan “CircuitLord” Juarez would like to know, too. “VR has sort of been stuck, where developers and studios can’t commit to larger Triple A style games because they’re WAY harder to make for VR and the playerbase is so small, but the playerbase is so small because there’s no large or Triple A games being made,” the mod’s developer tells The Verge. “I think mods like this are a great way to jumpstart and hopefully break that loop to show studios how much fun VR really can be.” In his Discord channel, Juarez is somewhat less diplomatic about his reasoning, saying things to his fans like: Juarez isn’t just a disgruntled VR fan — he’s a developer who had a front-row seat to the trend of kids becoming the primary customer for VR games. He says he worked on Gorilla Tag, the poster child for kids messing around in VR, as well as Zenith: The Last City, Synth Riders, and the 2020 Virtual Desktop competitor Desktop Portal. He also watched Meta inexplicably shut down one of his favorite games, Echo Arena, and he quit his job at VR studio Another Axiom after its Echo spiritual successor Orion Drift didn’t turn into the game he’d hoped for. This year, he started his own solo studio, Iteration Three, to build his own Echo Arena successor, No Shot. This fall, he decided to try a side project: Could he make existing games more playable in VR? In August, he revealed a VR mod for the popular Big Walk. In September, he announced he would do Titanfall 2 if he could manage to make it feel like “a true native VR experience, without any jank.” “I played Titanfall 2 years and years ago when it came out originally, always was a huge fan of it,” he tells me, and says a lot of the work was building custom interactions and reload animations, in addition to the obvious stuff like giving you a stereo 3D perspective and adding VR hands: A specific example: the manual reloading for guns. The process involved basically splitting apart the existing animations and sound effects in the game, and hooking them up to VR related triggers instead. When you grab the magazine from over your shoulder, it positions it in your palm and plays a given animation on that hand to make it look like it’s grasping it. The gun itself has custom hitboxes I’ve set up for grabbing different parts of it, for example where you grab to rack the slide. This all took a TON of iteration given the large amount of guns in the game. He says he’s most proud of some special interactive moments he added that were previously cutscenes, like this one (warning, spoilers!): But, he tells me, Titanfall 2’s developers deserve the credit. “A lot of the praise I’m getting really should be going to the developers who crafted the amazing environments, sound effects, and story that so many people are now experiencing for the first time.” He’s also standing on the shoulders of Titanfall 2 community mod loader Northstar, which he uses to help integrate the mod. And yes, he did take advantage of AI too. While “basically all the game design stuff is still down to me,” he says, “AI has been really helpful on the reverse engineering side of things and for speeding up the development process in general.” Juarez’s work on Titanfall isn’t done: He says he’s getting a ton of requests to add multiplayer modes with private VR servers and is currently working on that. He’s also still patching the single-player campaign, which isn’t yet a perfect experience; occasionally I’ll try to look through a scope that’ll display as black rather than see-through, and I’ve had a few issues trying to pick up certain difficult-to-reach weapons. He recently tweaked it so Titans will turn when you aim your weapon far enough to the right or left, instead of just with a joystick. If you’ve got a PC and headset and want to try it for yourself, it shouldn’t be hard. Titanfall 2 is currently (and frequently) on sale for just $5, and the campaign is already quite playable on relatively modest PC hardware. Some say they’ve gotten it to run with an Nvidia GTX 1080 or a modern midrange RTX 3060 laptop GPU, since Titanfall 2 was always a very well-optimized PC game. You also don’t have to jump through many hoops to install it. The mod works with all three PC versions of Titanfall 2 and does practically everything in three clicks, though you do need to sign into the EA app and make sure SteamVR is running before you put your headset on. When I put on Meta’s VR Glasses, and when I reviewed the Steam Frame, I couldn’t help thinking: I wish we’d had this awesome new hardware before companies stopped investing in VR games! But now that we live in the recomp revolution, when pissed-off gamers and laid-off developers are channeling that energy (and a lot of AI) into breaking games free of their shackles, I wonder if this is the future of VR. Labors of love, and spite, standing atop the shoulders of those who came before. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Doxxing Jane Doe
2026-10-07

Doxxing Jane Doe

The Verge | 2026-10-07

Visit Original Website
X and Substack aren’t doing anything to stop users from harassing women they believe are connected to the rape case — and the women who aren’t.﻿ If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. X and Substack aren’t doing anything to stop users from harassing women they believe are connected to the rape case — and the women who aren’t.﻿ If you buy something from a link, The Verge may earn a commission. See our ethics statement. Who is Jane Doe? Online vigilantes have tried to expose the Cornell University student who filed a lawsuit accusing seven fraternity members of gang rape, and in so doing have invited a torrent of abuse on several other women. Her lawyers say three women have been falsely identified and harassed — and the real Jane Doe is being inundated with threats, too. “She’s not doing well,” her attorney Thomas Guiffra told CNN on Sunday. “She’s under siege on social media, where people are trying to identify her.” The fact several women are now facing a torrent of online abuse in connection to Jane Doe’s lawsuit isn’t surprising. Targeted harassment and intimidation campaigns were a hallmark of the backlash to #MeToo. These targeted harassment campaigns were once organized on websites like Kiwi Farms and 4chan. But the effort to unmask Jane Doe — and to terrorize any woman they believe to be Jane Doe — is happening right in the open, on mainstream social media platforms that aren’t doing anything to stop it. Jane Doe’s identity isn’t a total secret: It’s known to her attorneys, the judge presiding over the case, the seven men she’s accusing of rape, and their attorneys, too. In civil cases, a plaintiff’s anonymity is ultimately up to the judge, who weighs several factors, including whether identifying the plaintiff by name will put them at risk of physical or mental injury. “In New York, there’s no automatic right to proceed anonymously,” said Annie Seifullah, a founding partner at the victims’ rights firm Incendii Law. Still, “the law recognizes that survivors’ lives are routinely put on trial, and that forcing them to sue under their real names would keep many victims of sexual violence from coming forward at all.” Similarly, journalistic ethics guides typically suggest showing sensitivity to victims of alleged sex crimes, and reporters will often allow people alleging abuse to do so anonymously, so long as their identity can be independently corroborated. The attempt to expose Jane Doe began on X, where Substack author and podcast host Michael Tracey — who is perhaps best known for his skepticism toward the allegations made about Jeffrey Epstein — solicited information about her. Tracey said he considered her identity fair game “since her lawsuit didn’t hesitate to name & shame 7 of her peers as gang-rapists.” Aidan Thompson, founder of the AF Post — a publication dedicated to articulating “the ideas, beliefs, and values of the America First Movement” — also asked for help identifying Jane Doe. “Beev, please help me discover the Cornell girl,” he posted on September 29th, referring to the “burnerverse,” a network of anonymous X accounts whose members discuss college sports and wage harassment campaigns against women with equal zeal. “The fact that people are trying so hard to dox her and are harassing her and others — women they just think might be her — underscores how serious her privacy interest really is,” said Katie McKay, a senior associate at C.A. Goldberg, a victims’ rights firm that specializes in tech-facilitated abuse. “Obviously the anonymity is necessary. It’s protecting her, to some extent, from even more harassment, even more targeting and retaliation.” X’s terms of service expressly prohibits users from sharing a person’s private information without their consent, and from threatening to do so; the site has also banned people who post the alleged names of pseudonymous users. But non-users’ names and photos aren’t considered “private information,” even if they can be used to dig up actual private details like their phone number or home address. X’s rules also say users “may not target others with abuse or harassment, or encourage other people to do so,” but the platform is flooded with posts disparaging women believed to be Jane Doe. X did not respond to a request for comment. On X, Thompson posted photos and biographical details of a woman he claimed was Jane Doe. Tracey later identified the same woman as Jane Doe on his Substack, claiming to have seen her name on court records. In fact, Tracey got the name from a court records aggregator — a website that, according to disinformation researcher Jared Holt, was registered in mid-September and seems to selectively post information about polarizing cases, like Clavicular’s and Lindsay Clancy’s. Despite Tracey’s repeated assertions that Jane Doe’s identity was a matter of public interest, many comments on his Substack post focused on the purported Jane Doe’s sex appeal and how this related to the veracity of her allegations. On X, dozens if not hundreds of people — ranging from anonymous accounts to prominent right-wing commentators — are now scrutinizing her appearance, making sexual comments about her, and disparaging her boyfriend. Anonymous accounts have circulated her Instagram and TikTok posts and made AI deepfakes of the woman Tracey and Thompson named. Substack, too, is known for its lax content moderation policies. The platform has taken a laissez-faire approach to publishing, and in 2023 infamously refused to remove or demonetize several Nazi publications from its website. Tracey has seemingly benefited from his posts about Jane Doe: Substack lists his newsletter as 66th on its politics leaderboard. Substack did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Most survivors don’t report and this is why. Tracey, for his part, deleted the post in which he claimed to have identified Jane Doe — not because of his shoddy sourcing but because he claimed to have made his point: High-profile litigants shouldn’t be allowed to be anonymous, especially when accusing others of rape. “That’s all I ever sought to accomplish here,” he wrote. “Not to impugn this young woman on a personal level. I have no animus toward her, and never have. I sincerely wish her the best, and hope her life is not defined by this calamity.” Tracey did not respond to a request for comment. Jane Doe isn’t anonymous to the court. “Underlying this is the belief that it’s somehow unfair to these defendants that she’s anonymous and they’re not. But they know who she is,” McKay said. “I don’t see a lack of accountability, even as a Jane Doe, in voluntarily subjecting your claims to the scrutiny of a court, with the subpoena power and the discovery power of the court.” Because the defendants know Jane Doe’s identity, their attorneys will be able to request access to her medical records, psychiatric records, and personal communications. “If the worry is that she wouldn’t be held accountable for any kind of false allegation, the whole process is designed to vet these allegations and only allow the claim to move forward — and her to potentially recover anything — if it’s deemed meritorious upon examination of all this evidence.” And regardless of their stated motivations for doing so, anyone who has spent any time on X since 2022 (or the internet more broadly) would know that identifying a woman as Jane Doe would inevitably result in that woman getting a torrent of abuse. Either Thompson and Tracey identified and directed harassment toward an alleged rape victim, or they directed harassment toward an unrelated woman in their quest to expose the identity of an alleged rape victim. These harassment campaigns reverberate beyond their intended targets, creating a broader culture of intimidation. “I’ve already had many clients call me to ask if they should be worried about being doxxed over their Jane Doe status,” said Seifullah, the victims’ rights attorney. “Most survivors don’t report and this is why.” Ironically, Tracey is now complaining that an online mob is not only going after his family but also targeting the new owners of his childhood home, to which he has no relation. Harassment of people who come forward with sexual assault allegations isn’t a new phenomenon, andbut McKay noted that stringent efforts were taken to protect the identity of Emily Doe, woman who accused Stanford swimmer Brock Turner of rape in 2015, and “I don’t recall much of a doxxing campaign,” McKay said — even after Emily Doe’s victim impact statement went viral. When Chanel Miller came forward four years later, the news of her identity was truly revelatory even to those who had criticized her during Turner’s high-profile trial. “I think the interest in ‘exposing’ victims is going to be magnified these days,” McKay said. Though it’s impossible to pinpoint what changed, McKay pointed to harassment campaigns directed at Amber Heard and E. Jean Carroll, two women who publicly accused famous men of rape. “Those women were publicly identified, and they were torn to shreds, viciously attacked by the public,” McKay said. “I don’t know if perhaps that’s created more of an interest or an expectation that every case is going to let observers who are non-litigants pick apart the victim, but it seems pretty clear to me that’s what they want to do here.” Just like the #MeToo movement encouraged women to share their stories, the attempt to expose Jane Doe has generated a new wave of solidarity. On TikTok and Instagram, hundreds of women responded to Tracey’s request for information about the alleged Cornell victim with an “I am Spartacus”-esque campaign in which they proclaim to be Jane Doe. Some women are even coming forward with their own stories of sexual assault. Sarah Lupica, a 24-year-old content creator with more than 1.5 million followers on TikTok, posted a video about being sexually assaulted when she was 16 years old. “For the longest time, I blamed myself for being in that situation,” Lupica told me. Until now, she said, she lacked the language to explain what happened to her. Lupica was 13 years old at the height of #MeToo and had no recollection of the movement. It didn’t reach her. The moment passed, the culture changed, and the abuse of women persisted in a culture that is even more hostile to survivors. For Lupica, #IAmJaneDoe is a way of not only sharing her own story but showing other women that they aren’t alone. Lupica said she felt emboldened by the amount of other women who were coming forward. “I was really touched by it, and the whole movement of saying ‘I am Jane Doe,’” she told me. “It’s saying you stand with her and with everyone who’s been scared to speak — survivors who never got justice and girls who are really trying to understand what happened to them.” A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Energy Dome has an unusual solution: massive batteries that use compressed carbon dioxide to store energy for when the grid needs it. Compressing gas to store energy isn’t new.
